In this section, we present our main theorem together with its corollaries. We also outline the strategy of the proof, which will be carried out in detail in the subsequent sections of the paper.

For any
\alpha\in\Omega
and
k\geq 0
, recall that the downset
\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}
is defined as

\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}:=\{\beta\in\Omega\mid\beta\prec\alpha\}.

Also recall that the set
\Phi_{\omega}
(resp.
\Phi_{k}
) is defined in Definition
6.8
as the smallest set of constructive ordinals containing
0
and closed under addition and the Veblen functions
\phi_{i}
(Definition
6.1
), for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
(resp. all
i<k
).

Definition 7.1
.

A downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
of ordinals is called
bounded
if there exists
k\geq 1
such that
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{k}
. For any bounded downset
\mathsf{A}
, define
l(\mathsf{A})=0
if
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}
. Otherwise, define
l(\mathsf{A})
to be the least
k\geq 1
such that
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{k}
.

Example 7.2
.

For the first example, observe that
\Psi_{\omega}\subseteq\Phi_{1}
is clearly bounded. Moreover, it is trivial that
l(\Psi_{\omega})=0
. As a second example,
\Phi_{k}
is trivially bounded for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
To compute
l(\Phi_{k})
, note that if
k=0
, then
\Phi_{0}=\{0\}\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}
. Hence,
l(\Phi_{0})=0
.
For
k\geq 1
, by Corollary
6.19
, we have
\Phi_{k}\nsubseteq\Phi_{k-1}
and
\Phi_{1}\nsubseteq\Psi_{\omega}
. It follows that
\Phi_{k}\nsubseteq\Psi_{\omega}
, and therefore
l(\Phi_{k})=k
. Moreover, note that
\Phi_{\omega}
is unbounded; otherwise, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Phi_{k+1}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}\subseteq\Phi_{k}
, which is impossible by Corollary
6.19
.
As a third example, we show that for any
\alpha\preceq\omega^{\omega}
, the downset
\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}
is bounded and
l(\mathsf{D}_{\alpha})=0
. Note that by the definition of
\omega^{\omega}=\langle\omega^{i+1}\rangle_{i}
, if
\beta\prec\alpha\preceq\omega^{\omega}
, by the properties of
\prec
, then
\beta\prec\omega^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
. Since
\omega^{k}\in\Psi_{\omega}
, Lemma
6.21
yields
\beta\in\Psi_{\omega}
. Thus,
\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}
, and so
\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}
is bounded and
l(\mathsf{D}_{\alpha})=0
.
As concrete examples we will use later, we have
l(\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{k}})=l(\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{\omega}})=0
for any
k\geq 1
.

The main theorem of this paper is the characterization of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
for any downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
such that
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
.

Theorem 7.3

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
be a downset of ordinals with
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
. Then:

(i)

If
\mathsf{A}
is bounded, then
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}=\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
.

(ii)

If
\mathsf{A}
is unbounded, then
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}=\mathrm{PR}
.

We will prove part
(i)
of Theorem
7.3
later. However, part
(ii)
follows as a consequence of part
(i)
, and hence we provide its proof now.

Proof of part
(ii)
.

First, note that since
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
such that
\alpha\nprec\omega
. By the dichotomy theorem (Theorem
6.24
), since
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
, there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that either
\alpha=o(r)
or
o(r)+\omega\preceq\alpha
. The first case is impossible, as
\alpha\nprec\omega
. Therefore,
o(r)+\omega\preceq\alpha
. Since
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}
is a downset, we conclude
o(r)+\omega\in\mathsf{A}
.

Second, define
\mathsf{A}_{k}=\mathsf{A}\cap\Phi_{k}
for any
k\geq 1
. First, by Lemma
6.9
,
\Phi_{\omega}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{k}
. Since
\Phi_{0}=\{0\}\subseteq\Phi_{1}
, we also have
\Phi_{\omega}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Phi_{k}
. Therefore, as
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
, it follows that
\mathsf{A}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathsf{A}_{k}
. Second, by Lemma
6.21
, each
\Phi_{k}
is a downset, and hence each
\mathsf{A}_{k}
is a bounded downset. Third, since
o(r)+\omega\in\Phi_{k}
for any
k\geq 1
, we have
o(r)+\omega\in\mathsf{A}_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
. Hence, as
o(r)+\omega\nprec\omega
, it follows that
\mathsf{A}_{k}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
. Therefore, we can apply part
(i)
of Theorem
7.3
to each
\mathsf{A}_{k}
.

Furthermore, note that the set
\{l(\mathsf{A}_{k})\mid k\geq 1\}
is unbounded; for otherwise, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
l(\mathsf{A}_{k})\leq N
for all
k\geq 1
. Then
\mathsf{A}_{k}\subseteq\Phi_{N}
by the definition of
l
, which implies
\mathsf{A}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathsf{A}_{k}\subseteq\Phi_{N}
, contradicting the assumption that
\mathsf{A}
is unbounded.

Now, since
\mathsf{A}_{k}\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{k+1}
and
\mathsf{A}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathsf{A}_{k}
, by Lemma
5.13
and part
(i)
of Theorem
7.3
, we have

\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}_{k}}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A}_{k})+2}.

Since the set
\{l(\mathsf{A}_{k})\mid k\geq 1\}
is unbounded, we have
\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A}_{k})+2}=\mathrm{PR}
, which implies
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}=\mathrm{PR}
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

The characterization provided in Theorem
7.3
is tight for any downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
of ordinals. The only aspect that requires further clarification is the condition
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
. This condition serves as a natural restriction, ensuring that
\mathsf{A}
is sufficiently large to support a meaningful recursion. Indeed, if
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, then predicative ordinal recursion operates only up to a fixed finite ordinal, effectively reducing predicative ordinal recursion to safe composition. In this case, the class loses its predicative ordinal recursion scheme entirely and becomes essentially the closure of basic functions under safe composition, and hence too weak to be of significant interest.

Some concrete instances of Theorem
7.3
are particularly noteworthy. First, we can apply the characterization to the classes
\Psi_{\omega}
,
\Phi_{k}
(for
k\geq 1
), and
\Phi_{\omega}
:

Corollary 7.5
.

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{\omega}}=\mathcal{E}_{2}
,

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k}}=\mathcal{E}_{k+2}
, for any
k\geq 1
, and

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{\omega}}=\mathrm{PR}
.

Proof.

First, note that none of the classes
\Psi_{\omega}
,
\Phi_{k}
(for
k\geq 1
), and
\Phi_{\omega}
is a subset of
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, since they all contain
\omega
. Then, it is enough to use Example
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

As consequences of Theorem
6.10
and Theorem
6.13
, we showed that
\Psi_{\omega}
,
\Phi_{k}
(for
k\geq 1
), and
\Phi_{\omega}
are the constructive counterparts of the classes of all set-theoretic ordinals below
\bm{\omega}^{\omega}
,
\bm{\phi}_{k}(\bm{0})
, and
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{0})
, respectively. Therefore, one can intuitively interpret Corollary
7.5
as stating that predicative ordinal recursion up to ordinals below
\bm{\omega}^{\omega}
,
\bm{\phi}_{k}(\bm{0})
, or
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{0})
corresponds to the classes
\mathcal{E}_{2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, and
\mathrm{PR}
, respectively, if
k\geq 1
. For instance, to construct all and only elementary functions (i.e., the functions in
\mathcal{E}_{3}
), one must use predicative ordinal recursion up to the ordinals below
\bm{\phi}_{1}(\bm{0})=\bm{\epsilon_{0}}
.

As a second remark, note that Corollary
7.5
can alternatively be interpreted as a characterization of the Grzegorczyk hierarchy via predicative ordinal recursion on the ordinals within the corresponding classes. This yields a structural and machine-independent characterization of the Grzegorczyk hierarchy, extending the Bellantoni–Cook characterization of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
to all levels of the hierarchy.

Another interesting special case of Theorem
7.3
arises when the downset
\mathsf{A}
is restricted to either
\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{\omega}}
or
\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{k}}
for
k\geq 2
. In these cases, we have:

Corollary 7.7
.

\mathrm{PredR}_{\omega^{k}}=\mathrm{PredR}_{\omega^{\omega}}=\mathcal{E}_{2}
, for any
k\geq 2
.

Proof.

First, note that for any
k\geq 2
, neither of the classes
\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{k}}
nor
\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{\omega}}
is a subset of
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, since both include
\omega
. Then, it is enough to use Example
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
.
∎

Corollary
7.7
states that if we extend predicative recursion from the set of natural numbers to any fixed finite product thereof, or even to the union of all such finite products, the resulting functions still remain computable in linear space.

Now, let us explain our strategy to prove part
(i)
of Theorem
7.3
. To that end, given any bounded downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
satisfying
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, we need to establish two claims:
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
. For each of these claims, we follow a strategy that will be outlined in the following in a rough manner.

For the first claim, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, using the assumption
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
and Theorem
6.24
, we can easily deduce that there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
o(r)+\omega\in\mathsf{A}
. Therefore, by Corollary
5.11
, all linear-space computable functions (i.e., the class
\mathcal{E}_{2}
) are contained in
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.
Hence, if
l(\mathsf{A})=0
, the claim follows immediately. For the case
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 1
, since
\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, the strategy is to show that any function in
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
is computable in space that is linear in a function from
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, and then to use the closure of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
under composition to obtain the desired function in
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.

To this end, recall that each function in
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
is computable in space bounded by a function in
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
. When
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
(resp.
l(\mathsf{A})=1
), since any function in
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
is bounded by some number of iterations of
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}
(resp. by the function
e(n)=2^{n}
), it follows that these functions are computable in space bounded by some number of iterations of
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}
(resp.
e
).
Moreover, recall from Example
5.4
that
G_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}
is essentially bounded by
G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}
if
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
, and that
G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}
(resp.
e
) is bounded by
G_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, if
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
(resp.
l(\mathsf{A})=1
). This strategy will be carried out in Section
8
.

For the reverse direction
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
, we consider two cases. If
l(\mathsf{A})=0
, then
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\Psi^{m}_{k}
, and if
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 1
, then
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\Phi^{m}_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}
, by Definition
7.1
, Lemma
6.12
, and Lemma
6.9
. In both cases, since
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
is continuous in
\mathsf{A}
by Lemma
5.13
, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi^{m}_{l}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l+3}
for all
k,m\geq 1
and
l\geq 0
.

For this purpose, we first introduce a suitable notation system for ordinals, encoding them as strings over the alphabet

\Sigma=\{0,1,(,),;,\bot\}
, which enables the simulation of the functions in

\mathcal{C}_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{m}_{l}}
) via the functions in

\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}^{\circ}
), i.e., the Grzegorczyk hierarchy over
\Sigma
.
Then, for numeral functions in
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}
(resp.
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi^{m}_{l}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l+3}
), we can return to the usual Grzegorczyk hierarchy by Remark
2.7
.

Up to this encoding, note that the basic functions in
\mathcal{C}_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{m}_{l}}
) are already available at
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
, and each
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\circ}
is closed under composition. Therefore, the main task is to simulate predicative ordinal recursion over the ordinals in
\Psi^{m}_{k}
(resp.
\Phi^{m}_{l}
) using length-bounded primitive recursion available in
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}^{\circ}
).
To explain, let
f
be defined by predicative ordinal recursion:

\displaystyle f(0,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=g(\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),

\displaystyle f(\mu+1,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{suc}}(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a}),

\displaystyle f(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{lim}}(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\mu_{q(\bar{n};)},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a}).

In computing
f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
, observe that the ordinal recursion effectively proceeds along the sequence of
q(\bar{n})
-predecessors of
\mu
, starting from
0
and culminating at
\mu
. Therefore, it suffices to compute

f(R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n})),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

as a function of
i\in\mathbb{N}
, which can be carried out via primitive recursion on
i
, using
R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
to determine the ordinal at each step, and
g
,
h_{\mathsf{suc}}
, and
h_{\mathsf{lim}}
for the base and recursive cases. Finally, setting
i=L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
yields
f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
, thus completing the simulation of ordinal recursion by primitive recursion.
Now, to show that
f
is in
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}^{\circ}
), it remains to verify that
R
and
L
belong to
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}^{\circ}
). Furthermore, by bounding the lengths of the values of
f
with a function in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}
), we can apply length-bounded primitive recursion (Theorem
2.8
) to conclude that
f
is in
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{l+3}^{\circ}
). We pursue this line of argument in Section
9
.

8
Simulation of the Grzegorczyk Hierarchy

In this section, we prove that
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
for any bounded downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
satisfying
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
.
As explained in Section
7
, a key component of our strategy is to establish an upper bound for
h_{i}
in terms of
G_{\phi_{i-2}(0)}
for all
i\geq 3
. To this end, we must analyze the behavior of the slow-growing hierarchy on the ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
. Recall that the ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
are generated from
0
using ordinal addition and the
\phi_{k}
functions. Moreover, the functions in the slow-growing hierarchy are additive with respect to the ordinal argument. Therefore, to understand the behavior of the slow-growing hierarchy on
\Phi_{\omega}
, it suffices to compute
G_{\phi_{k}(\alpha)}(n)
in terms of
G_{\alpha}(n)
.

In this section, we will also obtain an
upper bound
for the length hierarchy on ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
.
Since the functions in the length hierarchy, like those in the slow-growing hierarchy, commute with addition on the ordinal argument, it suffices to bound
L_{\phi_{k}(\alpha)}(n)
in terms of
L_{\alpha}(n)
. As these two tasks are structurally similar, we treat them together in this section, although the upper bound on the length hierarchy will only be needed later in Section
9
.

To accomplish these tasks, we introduce two families of functions
\{H_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{Q_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
to simulate the computation of
G_{\phi_{k}(\alpha)}(n)
(resp.
L_{\phi_{k}(\alpha)}(n)
) in terms of
G_{\alpha}(n)
(resp.
L_{\alpha}(n)
).
To this end, consider the following numeral functions:

\displaystyle H_{0}(x,y):=(x+2)^{y},\quad\quad H_{k+1}(x,0):=H_{k}^{(x)}(x,0)+1

\displaystyle H_{k+1}(x,y+1):=H_{k}^{(x)}(x,H_{k+1}(x,y)+1)+1

\displaystyle Q_{0}(x,y):=(x+1)^{y},\quad\quad Q_{k+1}(x,0):=Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,0)

\displaystyle Q_{k+1}(x,y+1):=Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)

Note that
Q_{k}^{(x)}
and
H_{k}^{(x)}
denotes
x
many iterations on the
second
argument of
Q_{k}
and
H_{k}
, respectively. Later, in Lemma
8.4
, we will see that

G_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)=Q_{k}(x,G_{\beta}(x))
and

L_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)\leq H_{k}(x,L_{\beta}(x))

for any
\beta\in\Omega
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We begin by establishing some basic properties of these functions.

Lemma 8.1
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

(i)

Q_{0}(0,y)=1
and
Q_{k+1}(0,y)=y
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

Q_{k}(x,y)\geq y
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x\neq 0
.

(iii)

H_{k}(x,y)\geq y
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
.

As a consequence of
(i)
and
(ii)
, we have
Q_{k}(x,y)\geq y
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
and any
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, the first part is clear as
Q_{0}(0,y)=1^{y}=1
. For the second part, we use induction on
y
. For
y=0
, we have
Q_{k+1}(0,0)=Q_{k}^{(0)}(0,0)=0
and for the induction step:

Q_{k+1}(0,y+1)=Q_{k+1}^{(0)}(0,Q_{k+1}(0,y)+1)=Q_{k+1}(0,y)+1=y+1

For
(ii)
, we prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=0
, since
x>0
, then
Q_{0}(x,y)=(x+1)^{y}\geq 2^{y}\geq y
, for any
y\in\mathbb{N}
. For the inductive step, we assume the claim for
k
and use an induction on
y
to show
Q_{k+1}(x,y)\geq y
. For
y=0
, as
0\leq Q_{k+1}(x,0)
, there is nothing to prove. For the inductive step, by the definition of
Q_{k+1}(x,y+1)
and the induction hypothesis for
k
and
y
, we have:

Q_{k+1}(x,y+1)=Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)\geq Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1\geq y+1

This completes the proof of
(ii)
. The proof for
(iii)
is similar to
(ii)
.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Q_{k}
and
H_{k}
are monotone in both of their arguments, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We only prove the claim for
Q_{k}
. The other is similar. To prove the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
, we use an induction on
k
. For
k=0
the claim is clear, as
Q_{0}(x,y)=(x+1)^{y}
is monotone. For the inductive step, we assume the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
to prove the monotonicity of
Q_{k+1}
. For that purpose,
it is enough to prove
Q_{k+1}(x,y)\leq Q_{k+1}(x,y+1)
and

Q_{k+1}(x,y)\leq Q_{k+1}(x+1,y)
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. First, observe that
Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,z)\geq z
, for any
z\in\mathbb{N}
. The reason is that if
x=0
, then

Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,z)=z
, by definition and if
x>0
, by Lemma
8.1
, we have
Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,z)\geq z
.
Now, using
z=Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1
, we get:

Q_{k+1}(x,y+1)=Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)\geq Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1\geq Q_{k+1}(x,y).

To prove
Q_{k+1}(x,y)\leq Q_{k+1}(x+1,y)
, we use an induction on
y
. For
y=0
, by Lemma
8.1
and the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
, we have:

Q_{k+1}(x+1,0)=Q_{k}^{(x+1)}(x+1,0)\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x+1,0)\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,0)=Q_{k+1}(x,0).

For the induction step, using Lemma
8.1
, the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
and the induction hypothesis, we have:

Q_{k+1}(x+1,y+1)=Q_{k}^{(x+1)}(x+1,Q_{k+1}(x+1,y)+1)

\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x+1,Q_{k+1}(x+1,y)+1)\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)=Q_{k+1}(x,y+1).\qed

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be a natural number. Then:

(i)

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
, if
x>0
then
Q_{k}(x,y)\leq Q_{k+1}(x,y)
.

(ii)

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
H_{k}(x,y)\leq H_{k+1}(x,y)
.

Proof.

We only prove
(i)
. Part
(ii)
is similar. For
(i)
, we use induction on
k
. For
k=0
, we use induction on
y
. For
y=0
, by
x\geq 1
and using Lemma
8.1
, we have

Q_{1}(x,0)=Q_{0}^{(x)}(x,0)=Q_{0}^{(x-1)}(x,Q_{0}(x,0))\geq Q_{0}(x,0).

For the induction step, first, notice that by definition,
Q_{0}(x,y)(x+1)=Q_{0}(x,y+1)
. Now, by
x\geq 1
, Lemmas
8.1
and
8.2
and the induction hypothesis, we have

Q_{1}(x,y+1)=Q_{0}^{(x)}(x,Q_{1}(x,y)+1)\geq Q_{0}(x,Q_{1}(x,y)+1)\geq Q_{0}(x,Q_{0}(x,y)+1)

=(x+1)^{Q_{0}(x,y)+1}\geq Q_{0}(x,y)(x+1)=Q_{0}(x,y+1).

This completes the case
k=0
. For the induction step, we assume the claim for
k
and prove it for
k+1
. We prove by induction on
y
. For
y=0
, by Lemma
8.2
and the induction hypothesis, we have:

Q_{k+2}(x,0)=Q_{k+1}^{(x)}(x,0)\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,0)=Q_{k+1}(x,0).

For the induction step for
y
, by Lemma
8.2
and the induction hypothesis, we have:

Q_{k+2}(x,y+1)=Q_{k+1}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+2}(x,y)+1)\geq Q_{k+1}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)

\geq Q_{k}^{(x)}(x,Q_{k+1}(x,y)+1)=Q_{k+1}(x,y+1).\qed

The following lemma provides the connection between
\phi_{k}(\beta)
and
\beta
that we have been seeking:

Lemma 8.4
.

G_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)=Q_{k}(x,G_{\beta}(x))
and

L_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)\leq H_{k}(x,L_{\beta}(x))
, for any
\beta\in\Omega
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently,
G_{\alpha}
is monotone, for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
.

Proof.

We only prove
L_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)\leq H_{k}(x,L_{\beta}(x))
. The equality for
G
is similar and can also be found in the proof of
[
32
, Prop. VIII.8.27]
.
We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=0
, as
L_{\omega}(n)=n+2\geq 2
, by Lemma
4.5
, we have:

L_{\phi_{0}(\beta)}(n)=L_{\omega^{\beta}}(n)\leq L_{\omega}(n)^{L_{\beta}(n)}=(n+2)^{L_{\beta}(n)}=H_{0}(n,L_{\beta}(n)).

For the induction step, let
k=l+1
and assume the claim for
l
. Now, by induction on
\beta
, we show that
L_{\phi_{k}(\beta)}(x)\leq H_{k}(x,L_{\beta}(x))
. For
\beta=0
, by the induction hypothesis for
l
and the monotonicity of
H_{l}
proved in Lemma
8.2
, we have

L_{\phi_{l+1}(0)}(n)=L_{\langle\phi_{l}^{i}(0)\rangle_{i}}(n)=L_{\phi_{l}^{n}(0)}(n)+1\leq H_{l}^{(n)}(n,L_{0}(n))+1=H_{l+1}(n,0).

For the successor
\beta=\gamma+1
, by the induction hypothesis for
l
and
\gamma
and the monotonicity of
H_{l}
proved in Lemma
8.2
, we have

L_{\phi_{l+1}(\gamma+1)}(n)=L_{\langle\phi_{l}^{i}(\phi_{l+1}(\gamma)+1)\rangle_{i}}(n)=L_{\phi_{l}^{n}(\phi_{l+1}(\gamma)+1)}(n)+1

\leq H_{l}^{(n)}(n,L_{\phi_{l+1}(\gamma)+1}(n))+1\leq H_{l}^{(n)}(n,H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma}(n))+1)+1

{=}H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma}(n)+1)=H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma+1}(n)).

For the limit
\beta=\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle
,
by the induction hypothesis for
\gamma_{n}
and the expansiveness of
H_{l}
, we have

L_{\phi_{l+1}(\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle)}(n)=L_{\langle\phi_{l+1}(\gamma_{i})\rangle}(n)=L_{\phi_{l+1}(\gamma_{n})}(n)+1\leq H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma_{n}}(n))+1

\leq H^{(n)}_{l}(n,H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma_{n}}(n))+1)+1=H_{l+1}(n,L_{\gamma_{n}}(n)+1)=H_{l+1}(n,L_{\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle}(n)).

This completes the proof of the first part. For the second part, i.e., the monotonicity of
G_{\alpha}
for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
, let
X
be the set of ordinals
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
for which
G_{\alpha}
is monotone. Since the constant zero function and each
Q_{i}
(for
i\in\mathbb{N}
) are monotone (Lemma
8.2
), and the sum of monotone functions is monotone, it follows from Lemma
4.5
and the first part that
X
contains
0
and is closed under the operations
\phi_{i}
and addition. Therefore,
X=\Phi_{\omega}
, which completes the proof of the second part.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Note that
G_{\alpha}
is not necessarily monotone for arbitrary
\alpha\in\Omega
. For instance, consider
\alpha=\langle 1,0,0,\ldots\rangle
, which is constantly zero except for the first element, which is one. In this case, we have
G_{\alpha}(0)=G_{1}(0)=1
, while
G_{\alpha}(n+1)=G_{0}(n+1)=0
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In the next lemma, we show the connection between iterating
Q_{k}
with respect to its second argument and
Q_{k+1}
. Moreover, we demonstrate the relationship between the two arguments of
Q_{k}
in a specific situation that we will need later.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
k
and
i
be natural numbers. Then:

(i)

Q_{k}^{2i}(2,2)\leq Q_{k+1}(2,i)
.

(ii)

Q_{k+1}(2,i)\leq Q_{k+1}(3i+2,0)
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, we use induction on
i
. For
i=0
, using the monotonicity of
Q_{k}(x,y)
in
k
(Lemma
8.3
) and in
x
and
y
(Lemma
8.2
), we have

Q_{k}^{0}(2,2)=2\leq 3=Q_{0}^{2}(2,0)\leq Q_{k}^{2}(2,0)=Q_{k+1}(2,0).

For the inductive step, by the induction hypothesis and the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
(Lemma
8.2
), we have

Q_{k}^{2i+2}(2,2)=Q_{k}^{2}(2,Q_{k}^{2i}(2,2))\leq Q_{k}^{2}(2,Q_{k+1}(2,i))

\leq Q_{k}^{2}(2,Q_{k+1}(2,i)+1)=Q_{k+1}(2,i+1),

which completes the proof.

For
(ii)
, since
Q_{k}^{j}(2,0)\leq Q^{j}_{k}(j,0)=Q_{k+1}(j,0)
for any
j\geq 2
by Lemma
8.2
, it is enough to prove that
Q_{k+1}(2,i)\leq Q_{k}^{3i+2}(2,0)
. To prove this claim, we use induction on
i
.
For
i=0
, by definition, we have

Q_{k+1}(2,0)=Q_{k}^{2}(2,0).

For the inductive step, first note that by the monotonicity of
Q_{k}(x,y)
in
k
(Lemma
8.3
), we have

y+1\leq 3^{y}=Q_{0}(2,y)\leq Q_{k}(2,y),

for any
y\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, using the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
(Lemma
8.2
) and the induction hypothesis, we obtain

Q_{k+1}(2,i+1)=Q_{k}^{2}(2,Q_{k+1}(2,i)+1)\leq Q_{k}^{2}(2,Q_{k}(2,Q_{k+1}(2,i)))

\leq Q_{k}^{3}(2,Q_{k}^{3i+2}(2,0))=Q_{k}^{3(i+1)+2}(2,0),

which completes the proof.
∎

Using the machinery developed thus far, we are now ready to provide the upper bound for
h_{k}
in terms of
G_{\phi_{k-2}(0)}
that we have been seeking. First, we use
Q_{k-2}(2,n)
instead.

Lemma 8.7
.

h_{k}(n)\leq Q_{k-2}(2,n)
, for any
k\geq 3
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=3
, recall that
h_{1}(n)=n^{2}+2
. Thus,
h_{1}(n)\geq 2
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
h_{1}(n)\leq n^{3}
for any
n\geq 2
. Therefore,
h_{2}(n)=h_{1}^{n}(2)\leq 3^{3^{n}}=Q_{0}^{2}(2,n)
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now, using the monotonicity of
h_{2}
and Lemma
8.6
, we reach

h_{3}(n)=h_{2}^{n}(2)\leq Q^{2n}_{0}(2,2)\leq Q_{1}(2,n).

For the inductive step, assume the claim for
k
. Then, by the induction hypothesis, the monotonicity of
h_{k}
, the expansiveness of
Q_{k}(2,-)
(Lemma
8.1
), and Lemma
8.6
, we have

h_{k+1}(n)=h_{k}^{n}(2)\leq Q^{n}_{k-2}(2,2)\leq Q^{2n}_{k-2}(2,2)\leq Q_{k-1}(2,n),

which completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.8
.

h_{k}(n)\leq G_{\phi_{k-2}(0)}(3n+2)
, for any
k\geq 3
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.7
, we have
h_{k}(n)\leq Q_{k-2}(2,n)
. Then, by Lemma
8.6
, we obtain
h_{k}(n)\leq Q_{k-2}(3n+2,0)
. Finally, since
G_{\phi_{k-2}(0)}(3n+2)=Q_{k-2}(3n+2,0)
by Lemma
8.4
, we obtain the desired bound.
∎

As the second part of the strategy for proving
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, as explained in Section
7
, we need to bound
G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}
(resp.
e(n)=2^{n}
) by
G_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, if
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
(resp.
l(\mathsf{A})=1
). For that purpose, we first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
k\geq 1
be a natural number. Then:

(i)

If
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, then
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq n+1
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

If
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}-\Psi_{\omega}
, then
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq 2^{n}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(iii)

If
\alpha\in\Phi_{k+1}-\Phi_{k}
, then
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
, for any
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, as
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, we have
\alpha\neq 0
. Therefore, there exist
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in
\Phi_{1}
such that
\alpha=\phi_{0}(\beta_{1})+\cdots+\phi_{0}(\beta_{m})
. Note that there exists
1\leq i\leq m
such that
\beta_{i}\neq 0
, because otherwise,
\alpha\prec\omega
. Pick
1\leq i\leq m
such that
\beta_{i}\neq 0
. As
\beta_{i}\in\Phi_{1}
, there exist
\{\gamma_{j}\}_{j=1}^{r}
in
\Phi_{1}
such that
\beta_{i}=\phi_{0}(\gamma_{1})+\cdots+\phi_{0}(\gamma_{r})
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
8.4
, we have:

G_{\beta_{i}}(n)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}G_{\phi_{0}(\gamma_{j})}(n)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}G_{\omega^{\gamma_{j}}}(n)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(n+1)^{G_{\gamma_{j}}(n)}\geq 1.

Therefore, by Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
8.4
, we reach

G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{0}(\beta_{i})}(n)=G_{\omega^{\beta_{i}}}(n)=(n+1)^{G_{\beta_{i}}(n)}\geq(n+1).

For
(ii)
, as
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}-\Psi_{\omega}
, we have
\alpha\neq 0
. Therefore, there exist
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in
\Phi_{1}
such that
\alpha=\phi_{0}(\beta_{1})+\cdots+\phi_{0}(\beta_{m})
. Again, note that there exists
1\leq i\leq m
such that
\beta_{i}\notin\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, because otherwise,
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}
. Pick
1\leq i\leq m
such that
\beta_{i}\notin\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
. Therefore, using
(i)
, Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
8.4
, we reach

G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{0}(\beta_{i})}(n)=G_{\omega^{\beta_{i}}}(n)=(n+1)^{G_{\beta_{i}}(n)}\geq(n+1)^{(n+1)}\geq 2^{n}.

For
(iii)
, let
X\subseteq\Phi_{k+1}
be the set of all ordinals
\alpha
such that either
\alpha\in\Phi_{k}
or
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
. To prove the claim, it suffices to show that
X=\Phi_{k+1}
. For this, we show that
X
contains
0
and is closed under addition and under
\phi_{i}
for any
i\leq k
.
Clearly,
0\in\Phi_{k}
and hence
0\in X
. For addition, let
\alpha,\beta\in X
. If both
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi_{k}
, then
\alpha+\beta\in\Phi_{k}
. Otherwise, assume that at least one of
\alpha
or
\beta
is outside
\Phi_{k}
. Since
\alpha,\beta\in X
, either
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
, or
G_{\beta}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
. Using the fact that

G_{\alpha+\beta}(n)=G_{\alpha}(n)+G_{\beta}(n),
from Lemma
4.5
, it follows that in either case

G_{\alpha+\beta}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
,
and thus
\alpha+\beta\in X
.
For closure under
\phi_{i}
for any
i\leq k
, let
\alpha\in X
. There are two cases: either
i=k
or
i<k
. If
i=k
, then we have the following by Lemma
8.4
and the monotonicity of
Q_{k}
(Lemma
8.2
), from which we conclude
\phi_{k}(\alpha)\in X
.

G_{\phi_{k}(\alpha)}(n)=Q_{k}(n,G_{\alpha}(n))\geq Q_{k}(n,0)=Q_{k}(n,G_{0}(n))=G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n).

If
i<k
, and
\alpha\in\Phi_{k}
, then clearly
\phi_{i}(\alpha)\in\Phi_{k}

and thus

\phi_{i}(\alpha)\in X
. Otherwise, if
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
, then by the expansiveness of
Q_{i}(n,-)
for
n\geq 1
(Lemma
8.1
), we have

G_{\phi_{i}(\alpha)}(n)=Q_{i}(n,G_{\alpha}(n))\geq G_{\alpha}(n)\geq G_{\phi_{k}(0)}(n),

which establishes
\phi_{i}(\alpha)\in X
.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to prove the direction of part
(i)
of Theorem
7.3
that we have been seeking in this section.

Theorem 8.10
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
be a bounded downset of ordinals such that
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
. Then,
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that, as
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
, by Theorem
6.24
, either every element in
\mathsf{A}
is of the form
o(r)
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
, or there exists an element
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
such that
o(r)+\omega\preceq\alpha
. The first case implies that
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
, which contradicts the assumption. Thus, as
\mathsf{A}
is a downset, there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
o(r)+\omega\in\mathsf{A}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, by Corollary
5.11
.

To prove
\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, we consider three cases. The case
l(\mathsf{A})=0
has already been established. We postpone the proof for the case
l(\mathsf{A})=1
to the end, as it follows with only minor adjustments from the last case
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
.

For
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
, let
f(n_{1},\ldots,n_{l})\in\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
to show that
f\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. First, by Theorem
2.5
, there exists a function
T_{f}\in\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
such that
f(\bar{n})
is computable in time
T_{f}({|\!|}{\bar{n}}{|\!|})
for any
\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Lemma
2.3
, there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that

T_{f}(\bar{m})\leq h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}^{M}\left(\max_{j}m_{j}\right)\quad\text{for all }\bar{m}\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}
is monotone and
{|\!|}{n_{j}}{|\!|}\leq n_{j}+1
for each
1\leq j\leq l
, we can conclude that
f(\bar{n})
is computable in time at most
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}^{M}(l+\sum_{j}n_{j})
.

Let
U
be a deterministic Turing machine that computes
f(\bar{n})
within at most
h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}^{M}(l+\sum_{j}n_{j})
steps. Define a function
g(i,\bar{n})
that returns the output of
U(\bar{n})
if the machine halts within
{|\!|}{i}{|\!|}
steps, and
0
otherwise. Since we use binary representations for numbers (including for the input
i
), it follows that

f(\bar{n})=g(i,\bar{n})\quad\text{for all }i\geq 2^{h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}^{M}(l+\sum_{j}n_{j})}.

It is clear that
g
is computable in linear space: it suffices to simulate
U
for at most
{|\!|}{i}{|\!|}
steps, which requires only
O({|\!|}{i}{|\!|})
cells. Thus,
g\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.

To conclude that
f\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, it now suffices to find a function
I(\bar{n})\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
such that
I(\bar{n})\geq 2^{h_{l(\mathsf{A})+1}^{M}(l+\sum_{j}n_{j})}
. Then, since
f(\bar{n})=g(I(\bar{n}),\bar{n})
and both
g,I\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, and
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
is closed under composition, we obtain
f\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. To find such an
I
, we show that:

Claim.
There are monotone functions
F,E\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
such that
F(n)\geq G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(n)
and
E(n)\geq 2^{n}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Using this claim and Corollary
8.8
, since addition and the constant functions are in
\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, and using the monotonicity of
F
and
E
, as well as the closure of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
under composition, we can find the desired function
I\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.

To prove the claim, first observe that, as
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
, by definition,
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})}
but
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}
. Thus, there exists
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
such that
\alpha\in\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})}-\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}
. Therefore, Lemma
8.9
yields
G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(n)\leq G_{\alpha}(n)
for all
n\geq 1
. This implies that
G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(n)\leq G_{\alpha}(n)+G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(0)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
F(n)=G_{\alpha}(n)+G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(0)
. By Lemma
8.4
, the function
F
is monotone. Moreover, since
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, we have
G_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, by Example
5.7
. Therefore, as the addition operation and any constant function belong to
\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, and
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
is closed under composition, we conclude that
F\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
.

To find
E
, as
l(\mathsf{A})-1\geq 1
, by Lemma
8.4
and Lemma
8.3
, if
n\geq 3
, we have:

F(n)\geq G_{\phi_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(0)}(n)=Q_{l(\mathsf{A})-1}(n,0)\geq Q_{1}(n,0)=

Q_{0}^{(n)}(n,0)\geq(n+1)^{(n+1)}\geq 2^{n}.

Therefore, if we define
E(n)=F(n)+4
, it follows that
E(n)\geq 2^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
F
is monotone,
E
is also monotone. Moreover, using a similar argument as in the case of
F
, we conclude that
E\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. This completes the proof of the claim, and hence the case
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 2
.

Finally, if
l(\mathsf{A})=1
, let
f\in\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}=\mathcal{E}_{3}
to show that
f\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. By Theorem
2.5
, there exists a function
T_{f}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
such that
f(\bar{n})
is computable in time
T_{f}({|\!|}{\bar{n}}{|\!|})
for any
\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Lemma
2.3
, there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that

T_{f}(\bar{m})\leq 2^{2^{\iddots^{\max_{j}m_{j}}}}\quad\text{for all }\bar{m}\in\mathbb{N}.

where the number of
2
’s is
M
. Then, with the same type of argument as above, it is enough to find a function
E(n)\geq 2^{n}
such that
E\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. For that purpose, as
l(\mathsf{A})=1
, by definition,
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{1}
but
\mathsf{A}\nsubseteq\Psi_{\omega}
. Therefore, there is
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
such that
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}-\Psi_{\omega}
. Hence, by Lemma
8.9
, we have
G_{\alpha}(n)\geq 2^{n}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, it is enough to set
E=G_{\alpha}
. As
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, we have
G_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
, by Example
5.7
. Moreover, as
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
, by Lemma
8.4
,
G_{\alpha}
is monotone which completes the proof.
∎

9
Simulation of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}

In this section, we follow the strategy outlined in Section
7
to prove that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
, where
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
is a bounded downset of ordinals. As discussed in Section
7
, this requires three main ingredients. First, in Section
9.1
, we present a notation system for the ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
. Second, in Section
9.2
, up to the encoding of ordinals, we locate the functions
R(i,\mu,n)
and
L_{\mu}(n)
within the Grzegorczyk hierarchy. Third, in Section
9.3
, we establish an upper bound for predicative ordinal functions in terms of functions in the Grzegorczyk hierarchy. Finally, in Subsection
9.4
, we combine these ingredients to prove the claim that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
.

9.1
A Notation System for
\Phi_{\omega}

Let
\Sigma:=\{0,1,(,),;,\bot\}
be the alphabet. We provide a notation system for the ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
in terms of strings over
\Sigma
.
First, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil
be the binary representation of
n
as a string over
\Sigma
. Then, recursively define the following notation (or code) for the ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
as strings in
\Sigma^{\ast}
, setting
\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil:=()
, and

\left\lceil{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\phi_{k_{i}}(\beta_{i})}\right\rceil:=(\left\lceil{k_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{\beta_{1}}\right\rceil)\cdots(\left\lceil{k_{m}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{\beta_{m}}\right\rceil),

where
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta_{i}\in\Phi_{\omega}
, for any
1\leq i\leq m
.
Note that, by Definition
6.8
and Theorem
6.16
, the zero ordinal cannot be written in the form
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\phi_{k_{i}}(\beta_{i})
, and for any non-zero ordinal in
\Phi_{\omega}
, such a representation exists and is unique. Hence, the coding function
\left\lceil{-}\right\rceil\colon\Phi_{\omega}\to\Sigma^{*}
is well-defined.

We define
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}
as the length of the string
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil\in\Sigma^{*}
, for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
. Moreover, recall that
{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}
denotes the length of the binary representation of
n
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
X\subseteq\Omega
be a set of ordinals. A function is called a
numeral function with ordinals in
X
if it takes as input only natural numbers and ordinals from
X
, and returns a natural number. Similarly, a function is called an
ordinal function with ordinals in
X
if it takes as input natural numbers and ordinals from
X
, and returns an output in
X
.

Remark 9.2
.

Observe that any downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Omega
is closed under the predecessor function
p
. Therefore, the restriction of
p
to
\mathsf{A}
, denoted by
p|_{\mathsf{A}}
is an ordinal function with ordinals in
\mathsf{A}
.

As both natural numbers and ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
are encoded as strings over
\Sigma
, any numeral or ordinal function with ordinals in
\Phi_{\omega}
naturally has a coded version over
\Sigma^{*}
. More precisely:

Definition 9.3
.

Given a numeral or ordinal function
f(\bar{\alpha},\bar{n})
with ordinals in
X\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
(Definition
9.1
), define its coded version
\llceil{f}\rrceil
over
\Sigma^{*}
by:

\llceil{f}\rrceil(\bar{u},\bar{v}):=\begin{cases}\left\lceil{f(\bar{\alpha},\bar{n})}\right\rceil&\exists\bar{\alpha}\in X\,\exists\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}\,(\bar{u}=\left\lceil{\bar{\alpha}}\right\rceil\wedge\bar{v}=\left\lceil{\bar{n}}\right\rceil),\\
\bot&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be either
\Phi_{\omega}
,
\Phi_{k}
, or
\Phi^{m}_{k}
for some
k\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
. Then:

(i)

Deciding whether a given string is the code of an ordinal in
\mathsf{A}
, and if it is, testing whether the ordinal is zero, a successor or a limit is possible in polynomial time.

(ii)

The function
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil:\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
is in
\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}
, where
p|_{\mathsf{A}}
is the restriction of the ordinal predecessor function to
\mathsf{A}
.

(iii)

There is an expansive polynomial function
P
with natural coefficients
such that

{|\!|}{p(\alpha,n)}{|\!|}\leq P({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|},{n})
, for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, checking whether a given string
w\in\Sigma^{*}
is the code of an ordinal in
\Phi_{\omega}
can be done by a recursive algorithm: first, we check whether
w=()
; if not, we verify whether
w=(u_{1};v_{1};\cdots;u_{l};v_{l})
, where each
u_{i}
is the binary code of a natural number
k_{i}
and each
v_{i}
is the code of an ordinal. It is clear that this algorithm runs in polynomial time. For
\Phi_{k}
, one also needs to add another check to each recursive call to ensure that
k_{i}<k
for any
1\leq i\leq l
. For
\Phi_{k}^{m}
, if
m=1
, since
\Phi^{1}_{k}=\Phi_{k}
, we already covered this case. If
m>1
, one also needs to add another check to each recursive call to ensure that for any
1\leq i\leq l
, either
k_{i}=k
and
v_{i}
is the code of an ordinal in
\Phi^{m-1}_{k}
, or
k_{i}<k
and
v_{i}
is the code of an ordinal in
\Phi^{m}_{k}
. It is clear that these modified algorithms also run in polynomial time.

For the second task, if the string is indeed the code of an ordinal
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, determining whether
\alpha
is zero, a successor, or a limit ordinal is straightforward. An ordinal is zero if and only if its code is
()
, and by Theorem
6.16
, it is a successor if and only if
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil
ends with
(\lceil 0\rceil;())
. Otherwise, it is a limit ordinal. Using
(i)
, all of these conditions can be checked in polynomial time.

For
(ii)
, using
(i)
, we present an algorithm to compute predecessors that runs in
\mathcal{E}_{3}
time. First, we check whether the given string is the code of an ordinal in
\mathsf{A}
. If it is not, we output
\bot
. Otherwise, we determine whether the input ordinal is zero, a successor, or a limit. In the first case, the output is
()
. In the successor case, computing
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil
from
\left\lceil{\alpha+1}\right\rceil
can be done by removing the rightmost
(\lceil 0\rceil;())
from the code
\left\lceil{\alpha+1}\right\rceil
. Therefore, the computation in these cases can be performed in time
{|\!|}{{\alpha}}{|\!|}^{O(1)}
.
The challenging part concerns the predecessor of limit ordinals. To address this case, we can easily use the definition of ordinal addition and Definition
6.1
to see that, for any limit ordinal
\alpha=\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle\in\Phi_{\omega}
, we have:

\alpha_{n}:=\begin{cases}\phi_{0}(\gamma)\cdot(n+1)&\text{if }\alpha=\omega^{\gamma+1}=\phi_{0}(\gamma+1)\\
\phi_{0}(\gamma_{n})&\text{if }\alpha=\omega^{\gamma}=\phi_{0}(\gamma),\text{ for }\gamma=\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle\text{ a limit}\\
\phi_{k}^{(n)}(0)&\text{if }\alpha=\phi_{k+1}(0)\\
\phi_{k}^{(n)}(\phi_{k+1}(\gamma)+1)&\text{if }\alpha=\phi_{k+1}(\gamma+1)\\
\phi_{k+1}(\gamma_{n})&\text{if }\alpha=\phi_{k+1}(\gamma),\text{ for }\gamma=\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle\text{ a limit}\\
\beta+\gamma_{n}&\text{if }\alpha=\beta+\gamma,\text{ for }\gamma=\langle\gamma_{i}\rangle\text{ a limit}\\
\end{cases}

This provides a recursive algorithm for computing
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil(\lceil\alpha\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
. To explain, let
\alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\phi_{k_{j}}(\gamma_{j})
. The main task is to compute the expression
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil(\lceil\phi_{k_{r}}(\gamma_{r})\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
. Observe that when
r=1
, this is exactly the required value, while for
r>1
, the desired code is obtained by appending
\lceil\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\phi_{k_{j}}(\gamma_{j})\rceil
to the left side of
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil(\lceil\phi_{k_{r}}(\gamma_{r})\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
.
Now, to compute
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil(\lceil\phi_{k}(\gamma)\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
, let
k=k_{r}
and
\gamma=\gamma_{r}
. Observe that
\lceil\phi_{k}(\gamma)\rceil
can be directly accessed from
\lceil\alpha\rceil
by extracting its last segment. There are two cases to consider.
If
\gamma
is zero or a successor, then computing
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil(\lceil\phi_{k}(\gamma)\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
is straightforward and can be done in time
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}n)
. This is because, in these cases, inspection of the explicit expression for
\alpha_{n}
listed above reveals that its length is bounded by
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}n)
.
If instead
\gamma
is a limit ordinal, the computation recurses into
\lceil\gamma\rceil
. Each step of the recursion just described takes
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}n)
time, and the recursion depth is
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|})
. Thus, the algorithm in this case runs in time
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}^{2}n)
.
Therefore, in terms of the representation length
{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}
, the computation takes
{|\!|}{{\alpha}}{|\!|}^{O(1)}2^{O({|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|})}
steps. Hence,
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil
is computable in
\mathcal{E}_{3}
time and thus belongs to the class
\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}
.

For
(iii)
, to define the polynomial
P
, observe from the analysis above that in each recursive step, we add at most
O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}n)
symbols. Therefore, we have
{|\!|}{p(\alpha,n)}{|\!|}\leq O({|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}^{2}n)
for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{\omega}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It is thus sufficient to define
P(x,n):=Cx^{2}n+x+n+D
, for sufficiently large constants
C,D\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Remark 9.5
.

As observed in the proof of Lemma
9.4
, some cases that arise in the computation of
p(\phi_{k+1}(\gamma),n)

invoke
n
iterations of
\phi_{k}
. As a result, the length of
\llceil{p}\rrceil(\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
becomes polynomial in
n
, and hence exponential in
{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}
. Therefore, the function
\llceil{p}\rrceil
cannot be computed in less than exponential time, and consequently,
\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}
is the lowest level of the Grzegorczyk hierarchy to include
\llceil{p}\rrceil
.

9.1.1
A linear-space notation system for
\Psi_{\omega}

A special case of the main objective of this section is to prove that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{\omega}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
This requires simulating the corresponding predicative ordinal recursion within the class
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the class of linear-space computable functions, is more resource-sensitive than the broader class of elementary functions on which our original ordinal notation system was based, we now require a more refined (i.e., linear-space efficient) notation system for the ordinals in
\Psi_{\omega}
.

Recall that, by Remark
6.20
, any non-zero
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}

has a unique normal form

\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}c_{i}
,
where
l\geq 1
,
the

c_{i}
’s are
non-zero finite ordinals,
and

p_{i}\neq p_{i+1}
for all

1\leq i<l
.
Therefore, for such ordinals, this presentation allows for a simpler and more compact notation system over the alphabet
\Sigma
.
We define this new notation as follows. If
\alpha=0
, we set
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}
as the string
()
. If
\alpha\neq 0
and has the normal form

\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}c_{i}
, define

\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}:=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l}}\right\rceil).

To ensure a uniform notation, we also extend the new notation
\left\lceil{-}\right\rceil_{E}
to numbers; that is, if
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we sometimes denote
\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil
by
\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil_{E}
. Moreover, define
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}
and
{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}_{E}
as the lengths of the strings
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}
and
\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil=\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil_{E}
, respectively.

Definition 9.6
.

Given a numeral or ordinal function
f(\bar{\alpha},\bar{n})
with ordinals in
X\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}
, define its coded version
\llceil{f}\rrceil_{E}
over
\Sigma^{*}
by:

\llceil{f}\rrceil_{E}(\bar{u},\bar{v}):=\begin{cases}\left\lceil{f(\bar{\alpha},\bar{n})}\right\rceil_{E}&\exists\bar{\alpha}\in X\,\exists\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}\,(\bar{u}=\left\lceil{\bar{\alpha}}\right\rceil_{E}\wedge\bar{v}=\left\lceil{\bar{n}}\right\rceil_{E}),\\
\bot&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be either
\Psi_{\omega}
,
\Psi_{k}
, or
\Psi^{m}_{k}
for some
k,m\geq 1
. Then:

(i)

Deciding whether a given string
w\in\Sigma^{*}
is of the form
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}
, for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, and, if it is, testing whether
\alpha
is zero, a successor, or a limit is possible in linear space.

(ii)

The function
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil_{E}:\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
is computable in linear space, where
p|_{\mathsf{A}}
is the restriction of the ordinal predecessor function to
\mathsf{A}
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, checking whether a given string
w\in\Sigma^{*}
is of the form
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}
, for some
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}
, can be done using the following algorithm: first, we check whether
w=()
; if not, we verify whether
w
is in the form
(\tau_{1};\tau_{2};\ldots;\tau_{2l})
for some
l\geq 1
, such that
\tau_{2i-1}
is the binary code of a number
p_{i}
, for any
1\leq i\leq l
, and
\tau_{2i}
is the binary code of a non-zero number
c_{i}
, for any
1\leq i\leq l
, and, second, whether

\tau_{2j-1}\neq\tau_{2j+1}
,
for any
1\leq j<l
. It is clear that the algorithm only checks the basic form, whether some strings are over
\{0,1\}
and start with
1
, along with verifying some equalities, and hence runs in linear space. For
\Psi_{k}
, we should also add another check to ensure that
p_{i}<k
for any
1\leq i\leq l
. For
\Psi^{m}_{k}
, we also need to check the existence of
1\leq r\leq l
such that
r\leq m
and
p_{j}<p_{j+1}
for any
r+1\leq j<l
. It is clear that these checks also run in linear space.

For the second task, if the string is indeed the code of an ordinal
\alpha\in\mathsf{A}
, determining whether
\alpha
is zero, a successor, or a limit ordinal is straightforward. An ordinal is zero if and only if its code is
()
, and by Theorem
6.16
, it is a successor if
\tau_{2l-1}=0
; and it is a limit otherwise. Using the previous part, all of these conditions can be checked in linear space.

For
(ii)
, first, we check in linear space whether
\alpha
is a code of an ordinal in
\mathsf{A}
. If it is not, we output
\bot
. Otherwise, we determine whether the ordinal is zero, a successor, or a limit. If it is zero, the predecessor is
()
. If it is a successor, then
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}
must be in the form

(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l}}\right\rceil)
, where
p_{l}=0
. Then, if
c_{l}=1
, we have
\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l-1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l-1}}\right\rceil)
and if

c_{l}\geq 2
, then
\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l}-1}\right\rceil)
.
It is clear that in these cases, the computation of the predecessor is possible in linear space.

If
\alpha
is a limit ordinal with the normal form
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}
, we have
p_{l}\geq 1
. Hence

p(\alpha,n)=\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}+\omega^{p_{l}}\cdot(c_{l}-1)+\omega^{p_{l}-1}\cdot(n+1).\qquad(*)

To compute
\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}
, it remains to transform
(*)
into normal form (gathering coefficients of adjacent terms with the same power, and removing a term whose coefficient now is
0
), and output its representation. If
c_{l}>1
, then
(*)
is already the normal form of
p(\alpha,n)
. Hence, we have

\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l}-1}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{p_{l}-1}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{n+1}\right\rceil).

If
c_{l}=1
, we can simplify
p(\alpha,n)
into

p(\alpha,n)=\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}+\omega^{p_{l}-1}\cdot(n+1).\qquad(**)

There are two cases to consider, either
p_{l-1}=p_{l}-1
or not. If
p_{l-1}\neq p_{l}-1
,
(**)
is already the normal form of
p(\alpha,n)
. Hence, we have

\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l-1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l-1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{p_{l}-1}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{n+1}\right\rceil).

Otherwise, the normal form of
p(\alpha,n)
is

p(\alpha,n)=\sum_{i=1}^{l-2}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}+\omega^{p_{l-1}}\cdot(c_{l-1}+n+1).

Therefore, we have

\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\left\lceil{p_{l-1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l-1}+n+1}\right\rceil)
.
It is clear that in computing
\left\lceil{p(\alpha,n)}\right\rceil_{E}
in this case, we essentially check some equalities and perform some additions. As these operations run in linear space,
\llceil{p|_{\mathsf{A}}}\rrceil_{E}
is computable in linear space.
∎

To control the computations that run on the ordinals in

\Psi_{\omega}
, along with their codes, a notion of norm is also required.

Definition 9.8
.

For any
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}
, if
\alpha=0
, define
|\alpha|=0
; and if
\alpha\neq 0
, define
{|}{\alpha}{|}:=\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}
, where
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}
is the normal form of
\alpha
.

For any natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the length of the binary representation of
n
, i.e.,
{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}
, is linearly related to
\log(n+1)
. More precisely,

\log(n+1)\leq{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}\leq\log(n+1)+1

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The same claim holds for ordinals in
\Psi^{m}_{k}
, where
k,m\geq 1
:

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}
be fixed numbers. Then,
there are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}
such that

\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)\leq{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}
and
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}\leq a\cdot\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)+b
,
for any
\alpha\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
.

Proof.

To make the proof easier to read, we first examine the conditions under which
a
and
b
satisfy the claim, and at the end, we show that the conditions have a solution.
First, if
\alpha=0
, then
|\alpha|=0
. Hence,
\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)=0\leq{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}
, and there is nothing to check. For
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}\leq a\cdot\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)+b
, since
{|\!|}{{0}}{|\!|}=2
, it is enough to set
b\geq 2
. If
\alpha\neq 0
, let

\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}c_{i}

be the normal form of
\alpha
. Hence,

\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil_{E}=(\left\lceil{p_{1}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{1}}\right\rceil;\cdots;\allowbreak\left\lceil{p_{l}}\right\rceil;\left\lceil{c_{l}}\right\rceil)
, which implies
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}=2l+1+\sum_{i}{|\!|}{p_{i}}{|\!|}+\sum_{i}{|\!|}{c_{i}}{|\!|}
.
For the first claim, first observe that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 2}
, we have
x+y\leq xy
.
Therefore, as
l\geq 1
, we obtain:

\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)\leq\log(1+\sum_{i}c_{i})\leq\log(\sum_{i}(c_{i}+2))\leq\log(\prod_{i}(c_{i}+2))=

\sum_{i}\log(c_{i}+2)\leq\sum_{i}\log(2(c_{i}+1))=l+\sum_{i}\log(c_{i}+1)\leq l+\sum_{i}{|\!|}{c_{i}}{|\!|}\leq{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}.

For the second claim, as
c_{i}\leq|\alpha|
and
p_{i}<k
for any
1\leq i\leq l
, and
l\leq k+m
(Remark
6.20
), we have:

{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}=2l+1+\sum_{i}{|\!|}{p_{i}}{|\!|}+\sum_{i}{|\!|}{c_{i}}{|\!|}\leq 2l+1+\sum_{i}(\log(p_{i}+1)+1)+\sum_{i}(\log(c_{i}+1)+1)

\leq 4l+1+\sum_{i}\log(p_{i}+1)+\sum_{i}\log(c_{i}+1)\leq C(m,k)+\sum_{i}\log(c_{i}+1)

\leq C(m,k)+l\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)\leq C(m,k)+(m+k)\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)

where
C(m,k)\geq 4l+1\geq 2
is a constant in terms of
m
and
k
.
Therefore, it is enough to set
a:=k+m
and
b:=C(m,k)
. Notice that
b\geq 2
as required for the case
\alpha=0
.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

{|}{{p(\alpha,n)}}{|}\leq{|}{{\alpha}}{|}+n
, for
any
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha=0
, we have
p(\alpha,n)=0
, and hence there is nothing to prove. If
\alpha\neq 0
, let
\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}
be the normal form of
\alpha
. Note that
c_{i}\geq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq l
.
If
\alpha
is a successor, then
p_{l}=0
, and hence

p(\alpha,n)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}\right)+(c_{l}-1).

Therefore,

{|}{p(\alpha,n)}{|}={|}{\alpha}{|}-1<{|}{\alpha}{|}\leq{|}{\alpha}{|}+n
.
If
\alpha
is a limit, then
p_{l}>0
, and hence

p(\alpha,n)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}\right)+\omega^{p_{l}}\cdot(c_{l}-1)+\omega^{p_{l}-1}\cdot(n+1).

Therefore,

{|}{p(\alpha,n)}{|}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}+n={|}{\alpha}{|}+n
.
∎

9.2
Computing
L_{\mu}(n)
and
R(i,\mu,n)

Recall (see Definition
4.4
) that
R^{b}(i,\mu,n)
is obtained by applying the
n
-predecessor function
i
times to
\mu
, and that
L_{\mu}(n)
is the least
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
R^{b}(l,\mu,n)=0
. We called the decreasing sequence
\{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)\}_{i=0}^{L_{\mu}(n)}
the sequence of
n
-predecessors of
\mu
, and defined
R(i,\mu,n)=R^{b}(L_{\mu}(n)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}i,\mu,n)
as the function that enumerates the sequence of
n
-predecessors of
\mu
in a forward manner (i.e., from
0
to
\mu
).

In this subsection, we complete the second step of the strategy outlined in Section
7
.
Up to the encoding of ordinals and numbers as strings over
\Sigma
, we show that the functions
R(i,\mu,n)
and
L_{\mu}(n)
lie in the appropriate level of the Grzegorczyk hierarchy. For this purpose, since
R
is defined in terms of
R^{b}
and
L
, it suffices to locate
R^{b}
and
L
within the hierarchy. Observe that
R^{b}
is defined by primitive recursion, and
L
by minimization.
Hence, to place these functions at the suitable level of the hierarchy, we first upper bound them by functions at this level, and then appeal to the closure of the Grzegorczyk classes under bounded recursion and bounded minimization (see Section
2
). We derive these required bounds separately for the restrictions of
R^{b}
and
L
to
\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
(with
k\geq 3
,
m\geq 1
) and to
\Psi_{k}^{m}
(with
k,m\geq 1
) in the following subsubsections.

9.2.1
The case
\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
, for
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1

In this subsubsection, we aim to show that
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
and
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil

belong to
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. As discussed above, this requires establishing upper bounds for
{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}
and
L_{\mu}(n)
.
We begin with the simpler case of
{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}
and then turn to the more involved case of
L_{\mu}(n)
.

Lemma 9.11
.

There is a monotone function
M_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
such that
{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\allowbreak\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|})
, for any
\mu\in\Phi_{\omega}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.4
, there is a monotone and expansive polynomial
P
such that
{|\!|}{{p(\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq P({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)
, for any
\mu\in\Phi_{\omega}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Define the function
M^{\prime}
by
M^{\prime}(i,x,y)=P^{i}(x,y)
, where
P^{i}
is the result of the
i
-iteration of
P
on the first argument. Note that
P^{0}(x,y)=x
and as
P
is monotone and expansive,
M^{\prime}
is also monotone in all arguments. Moreover, as
P\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
, by Lemma
2.3
.
Now, by induction on
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we prove

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq M^{\prime}(i,{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)
.
For
i=0
, we have

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(0,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}={|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}=P^{0}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)=M^{\prime}(0,{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)
.
For the inductive step, assume the claim for
i
. We know that

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i+1,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}={|\!|}{{p(R^{b}(i,\mu,n),n)}}{|\!|}\leq P({|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|},n)\leq P(P^{i}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n),n)=M^{\prime}(i+1,{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)
. This establishes
{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq M^{\prime}(i,{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n)
. It remains to obtain the bound in terms of
{|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|}
and
{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}
instead of
i
and
n
.
Define

M_{R}(j,x,y):=M^{\prime}(2^{j},\allowbreak x,2^{y})
and notice that
M_{R}
is monotone and
M_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
. Finally, as
M^{\prime}
is monotone, we reach

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|})
, for any
\mu\in\Phi_{\omega}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

To provide an upper bound for
L_{\mu}(n)
, we first need to prove some basic properties. First, as
L_{\phi_{k}(\mu)}(n)
is controlled by the function
H_{k}
and
L_{\mu}(n)
(Lemma
8.4
), our first task is clearly to locate the functions
\{H_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
in the Grzegorczyk hierarchy.

Lemma 9.12
.

H_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, for any
k\geq 3
and
i\leq k-3
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by an induction on
k
. For
k=3
, as
H_{0}
is clearly elementary, it is in
\mathcal{E}_{3}
and hence there is nothing to prove. For the induction step, assume the claim for
k
to prove it for
k+1
. Let
i\leq k-2
. If
i=0
, similar to the base case, i.e.,
k=0
, there is nothing to prove. If
i\geq 1
, let
i^{\prime}=i-1
. Note that
i^{\prime}\leq k-3
. Thus, by the induction hypothesis,
H_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. By Lemma
2.3
, the function
H_{i^{\prime}}^{(x)}
is in
\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
.
Since
H_{i}=H_{i^{\prime}+1}
is defined by a primitive recursion from
H_{i^{\prime}}^{(x)}\in\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
, if we show that it is bounded by a function in
\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
, we obtain
H_{i^{\prime}+1}\in\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
.

Now, since
H_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, by Lemma
2.3
, there is a constant
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
H_{i^{\prime}}(x,y)\leq E(\max\{x,y\})
, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
, where
E=h_{k-1}^{M}
. As
h_{k-1}
is strictly monotone and expansive, so is
E
. Therefore, by induction on
r\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
E^{(r)}(x)
is expansive and strictly monotone in
x
. Since
E^{(r)}(x)
is always a natural number, we obtain

E^{(r)}(x)+y\leq E^{(r)}(x+y)
,
for all
r,x,y\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, by an induction on
r\in\mathbb{N}
and the expansiveness of
E^{(j)}
’s, we can show that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
H_{i^{\prime}}^{(r)}(x,y)\leq E^{(r)}(\max\{x,y\})
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let
F(x,y):=E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y+1))
. As
E\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we have
F\in\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
, by Lemma
2.3
. Therefore, it is enough to show that
H_{i}(x,y)\leq F(x,y)
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. To prove this claim, we proceed by induction on
y
.
For the base case, since
H_{i^{\prime}}(x,0)\leq E(x)
, we have

H_{i}(x,0)=H_{i^{\prime}}^{(x)}(x,0)+1\leq E^{(x)}(x)+1\leq E^{(x)}(x+1)=F(x,0).

For the induction step, by the induction hypothesis,
H_{i}(x,y)\leq F(x,y)
. Thus,

H_{i}(x,y)+1\leq F(x,y)+1=E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+1}))+1

\leq E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+1}))+(y+1)

\leq E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+1})+(y+1))=E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2})).

Thus,
H_{i}(x,y)+1\leq E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2}))
.
Hence, using the monotonicity of
E
, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

H_{i^{\prime}}^{(r)}(x,H_{i}(x,y)+1)\leq E^{(r)}\big(\max\{x,H_{i}(x,y)+1\}\big)\leq E^{(r)}(x+H_{i}(x,y)+1)

\leq E^{(r)}\big(E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2}))+x\big)\leq E^{(r)}\big(E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2})+x)\big).

Therefore, putting
r=x
, we have:

H_{i}(x,y+1)=H_{i^{\prime}}^{(x)}(x,H_{i}(x,y)+1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+1}\leq E^{(x)}\big(E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+x})\big){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+1}

\leq E^{(x)}\big(E^{x(y+1)}((y+1)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+x+1})\big)

\leq E^{x(y+2)}((y+2)(x+y{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+2}))=F(x,y+1).

Therefore,
H_{i}(x,y)\leq F(x,y)
, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. This implies
H_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
.
∎

Now, to present the promised upper bound functions for
L_{\mu}(n)
, for each
k\geq 0
, define the family
\{J_{m}^{k}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of numeral functions by:

\displaystyle\,J_{0}^{0}(x,n,y):=y,

\displaystyle\,J_{m+1}^{0}(x,n,y):=x\cdot(n+2)^{J_{m}^{0}(x,n,y)},

\displaystyle\,J_{0}^{k+1}(0,n,y):=y,

\displaystyle J_{0}^{k+1}(x+1,n,y):=(x+1)\cdot H_{k}\big(n,(x+1)\cdot J_{0}^{k+1}(x,n,y)\big),

\displaystyle\,J_{m+1}^{k+1}(x,n,y):=x\cdot J_{0}^{k+1}\big(x,n,H_{k+1}\big(n,J_{m}^{k+1}(x,n,y)\big)\big).

In the following, we establish some properties of this family.

Lemma 9.13
.

(i)

J_{m}^{k}
is monotone in all arguments, for any
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

For any
m,k,x,n,y\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

x\cdot H_{k+1}(n,J_{m}^{k+1}(x,n,y))+x\cdot H_{k}(n,J_{m+1}^{k+1}(x,n,y))\leq J_{m+1}^{k+1}(x+1,n,y).

Proof.

For
(i)
, first, recall that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by Lemma
8.2
, the function
H_{k}
is monotone in both arguments. Now, if
k=0
, by induction on
m
, one can easily prove the monotonicity of
J^{0}_{m}
, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For
k>0
, we prove the claim by induction on
m
. If
m=0
, the monotonicity of
J_{0}^{k}
in
n
and
y
is trivial by the monotonicity of
H_{k-1}
. For its monotonicity in
x
, we have to show that
J^{k}_{0}(x+1,n,y)\geq J^{k}_{0}(x,n,y)
for any
x,n,y\in\mathbb{N}
. For that purpose, it is enough to use
H_{k-1}(a,b)\geq b
from Lemma
8.1
to obtain:

J^{k}_{0}(x+1,n,y)=(x+1)\cdot H_{k-1}\big(n,(x+1)\cdot J_{0}^{k}(x,n,y)\big)

\geq H_{k-1}\big(n,(x+1)\cdot J_{0}^{k}(x,n,y)\big)\geq(x+1)\cdot J_{0}^{k}(x,n,y)\geq J_{0}^{k}(x,n,y).

This completes the case
m=0
. The induction step is trivial by the monotonicity of
H_{k}
and
J_{0}^{k}
.

For
(ii)
, by the definition of
J_{0}^{k+1}
and the monotonicity of
J_{0}^{k+1}
,
J_{m}^{k+1}
, and
H_{k+1}
, we have:

\displaystyle x\cdot H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x,n,y))

\displaystyle=x\cdot J^{k+1}_{0}(0,n,H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x,n,y)))

\displaystyle\leq x\cdot J^{k+1}_{0}(x+1,n,H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x+1,n,y)))\quad(*)

and using the definition of
J^{k+1}_{m+1}
, we also have:

\displaystyle x\cdot H_{k}(n,J^{k+1}_{m+1}(x,n,y))

\displaystyle\leq(x+1)\cdot H_{k}(n,(x+1)\cdot J^{k+1}_{0}(x,n,H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x,n,y))))

\displaystyle=J^{k+1}_{0}(x+1,n,H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x,n,y)))

\displaystyle\leq J^{k+1}_{0}(x+1,n,H_{k+1}(n,J^{k+1}_{m}(x+1,n,y))).\quad(**)

Therefore, using the definition of
J^{k+1}_{m+1}
,
the sum of the right sides of
(*)
and
(**)
is upper bounded by
J^{k+1}_{m+1}(x+1,n,y)
.
∎

As
J^{k}_{m}
’s are intended to play the role of upper bounds for
L
, we clearly need to locate them in the Grzegorczyk hierarchy.

Lemma 9.14
.

J^{k-3}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, for any
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 0
.

Proof.

If
k=3
, by induction on
m
, it is clear that
J_{m}^{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
. If
k\geq 4
, we prove the claim by induction on
m
. For
m=0
, we have to prove
J^{k-3}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. As
J^{k-3}_{0}
is defined by primitive recursion, using a composition of
H_{k-4}
and polynomials, and since
H_{k-4}\in\mathcal{E}_{k-1}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}
by Lemma
9.12
, we only need to provide an upper bound for
J_{0}^{k-3}
that is also in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. For that purpose, define

E(x,n,z):=(x+1)\cdot H_{k-4}(n,(x+1)\cdot z).

Denote
u
iterations of
E
on its third argument by
E^{(u)}
, i.e.,
E^{(0)}(x,n,z)=z
and
E^{(u+1)}(x,n,z)=E(x,n,E^{(u)}(x,n,z))
. As
H_{k-4}\in\mathcal{E}_{k-1}
, we have
E\in\mathcal{E}_{k-1}
, and hence its iteration, i.e.,
E^{(u)}(x,n,z)
, is in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
by Lemma
2.3
. Therefore, it is enough to prove
J^{k-3}_{0}(x,n,y)\leq E^{(x)}(x,n,y)
, for any
x,n,y\in\mathbb{N}
.

We prove this claim by induction on
x
. For
x=0
, we clearly have
J^{k-3}_{0}(0,n,y)=y=E^{(0)}(0,n,y)
. For the induction step, by the induction hypothesis and the monotonicity of
H_{k-4}
proved in Lemma
8.2
, we have

J^{k-3}_{0}(x+1,n,y)\leq(x+1)\cdot H_{k-4}(n,(x+1)\cdot E^{(x)}(x,n,y))=E^{(x+1)}(x,n,y).

Therefore, for any
x,n,y\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
J^{k-3}_{0}(x,n,y)\leq E^{(x)}(x,n,y)
. This completes the proof of the case
m=0
in showing that
J_{m}^{k-3}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

For the inductive step, note that
J^{k-3}_{m+1}
is defined by composition from
J^{k-3}_{0}
,
H_{k-3}
,
J^{k-3}_{m}
, and a polynomial. By the induction hypothesis, we have
J^{k-3}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, and we have just proved that
J^{k-3}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Moreover, by Lemma
9.12
,
H_{k-3}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Therefore, since
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is closed under composition and contains all polynomials, the claim follows for
m+1
.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove the promised upper bound:

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
. Then,

L_{\mu}(n)\leq J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0)
, for any
\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

If
k=3
, we prove the claim by induction on
m
. If
m=1
, then
\mu=0
and the claim holds as
L_{0}(n)=0
. For the inductive step, let
m\geq 2
and assume the claim for
m-1
to prove it for
m
. If
\mu=0
, again there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, as
\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}=\Phi_{0}^{m}
, we can write
\mu
as
\sum_{i=1}^{l}\phi_{0}(\gamma_{i})
, where
\gamma_{i}\in\Phi^{m-1}_{k-3}=\Phi_{0}^{m-1}
, for any
1\leq i\leq l
. As
m\geq 2
, by the induction hypothesis,
L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)\leq J_{m-2}^{0}({|\!|}{{\gamma_{i}}}{|\!|},n,0)
, for any
1\leq i\leq l
.
Therefore, using the monotonicity of
J^{0}_{m-2}
from Lemma
9.13
, we reach
L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)\leq J_{m-2}^{0}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0)
, for any
1\leq i\leq l
.
By Lemmas
4.5

and
8.4
,
L_{\mu}(n)
is upper bounded by

\sum_{i=1}^{l}H_{0}(n,L_{\gamma_{i}}(n))=\sum_{i=1}^{l}(n+2)^{L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)}\leq{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}\cdot(n+2)^{J^{0}_{m-2}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0)}\leq J^{0}_{m-1}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0).

This completes the proof of the case
k=3
. For
k\geq 4
, we prove the claim by induction on
{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}
. If
\mu=0
, again, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, as
\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
, we can write
\mu
as
\sum_{i\in I}\phi_{k_{i}}(\gamma_{i})
, where
I
is non-empty,
k_{i}\leq k-3
, for any
i\in I
and if
k_{i}=k-3
, then
\gamma_{i}\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m-1}
and if
k_{i}<k-3
, we have
\gamma_{i}\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
. Note that as
I
is non-empty,
{|\!|}{{\gamma_{i}}}{|\!|}\leq{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1
, for any
i\in I
. Define
J=\{i\in I\mid k_{i}=k-3\}
and
K=\{i\in I\mid k_{i}<k-3\}
.
Therefore, by Lemmas
4.5
,
8.4
, and
8.3
, we have:

L_{\mu}(n)\leq\sum_{i\in I}H_{k_{i}}(n,L_{\gamma_{i}}(n))\leq\sum_{i\in J}H_{k-3}(n,L_{\gamma_{i}}(n))+\sum_{i\in K}H_{k-4}(n,L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)).

As
{|\!|}{{\gamma_{i}}}{|\!|}\leq{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1
, by the induction hypothesis and the monotonicity of
J^{k-3}_{m-1}
and
J^{k-3}_{m-2}
(Lemma
9.13
), we have
L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)\leq J_{m-2}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0)
for any
i\in J
and
L_{\gamma_{i}}(n)\leq J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0)
for any
i\in K
. Therefore,

L_{\mu}(n)\leq\sum_{i\in J}H_{k-3}(n,J_{m-2}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0))+\sum_{i\in K}H_{k-4}(n,J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0)).

As the cardinal of
I
is bounded by
{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1
, we reach

L_{\mu}(n)\leq({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1)(H_{k-3}(n,J_{m-2}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0))+H_{k-4}(n,J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}-1,n,0))).

Finally, by Lemma
9.13
, part
(ii)
, we get
L_{\mu}(n)\leq J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0)
.
∎

Finally, we have gathered all the necessary ingredients to conclude that both

\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
and
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
belong to
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
For completeness, we also included the function
\llceil{G|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
, as computing
G_{\mu}(n)
uniformly in
\mu
may be of independent interest.

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
. Then the functions
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil
,
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil
, and
\llceil{G|_{\Phi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil
all belong to
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Before we begin, recall from Lemma
9.4
that one can decide in polynomial time whether a string in
\Sigma^{\ast}
encodes an ordinal in
\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
, and, if so, whether the encoded ordinal is zero, a successor, or a limit. Moreover, recall from the same Lemma that
\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}
, and since
k\geq 3
, we have
\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

First, we show that
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
Define a function
I(w,z)
that outputs
w
if there exist
\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
w=\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil
and
z=\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil
, and outputs
\bot
otherwise. By the above discussion,
I
is computable in polynomial time and hence
I\in\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
Now, by Theorem
2.8
, it suffices to observe that
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
is defined by length-bounded recursion from functions in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
using a bounding function in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. For this purpose, notice the equality:

\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(v,w,z)=\begin{cases}I(w,z)&\text{if }v=\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil,\\
\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,w,z),z)&\text{if }v=\left\lceil{i+1}\right\rceil,\\
\bot&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

which is the encoded version of the recursive definition of
R^{b}
and is therefore straightforward to prove. Since
I
and
\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
are both in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, the only remaining task is to check the bound. For this, by Lemma
9.11
, there exists a monotone function
M_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}),

for any
i,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
. Since the output of
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
is either
\bot
or
\left\lceil{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}\right\rceil
, we obtain

{|\!|}{{\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(v,w,z)}}{|\!|}\leq 1+M_{R}({|\!|}{{v}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{w}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{z}}{|\!|}),

for any
v,w,z\in\Sigma^{*}
. Therefore,
since
1+M_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we get the intended upper bound.
Hence, by closure under length-bounded primitive recursion (Theorem
2.8
), we conclude that
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Now we show that
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. For this purpose, by Theorem
2.8
, it suffices to observe that
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
is essentially defined by bounded
\Sigma
-minimization from a function in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
using a bounding function in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.
For the bound, define

M_{L}(x,y)=J_{m-1}^{k-3}(x,2^{y},0),

and note that
M_{L}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
by Lemma
9.14
. Moreover, by Lemma
9.15
and the monotonicity of
J_{m-1}^{k-3}
from Lemma
9.13
, we have

L_{\mu}(n)\leq J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},n,0)\leq J_{m-1}^{k-3}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},2^{{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}},0)=M_{L}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}),

for any
\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Next, define

L_{R}(v,w):=\texttt{Min}\;\,i\leq M_{L}({|\!|}{{v}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{w}}{|\!|})\,[\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,v,w)=\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil].

We have already shown that
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
and
M_{L}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Therefore, closure under
\Sigma
-bounded minimization (Theorem
2.8
) implies that
L_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
Finally, observe the equality

\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(v,w)=\begin{cases}L_{R}(\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)&\exists\mu\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}\,\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\,(v=\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil\wedge w=\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil),\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

which is straightforward to verify. By the discussion at the beginning of the proof, deciding between the cases in the previous equality can be done in polynomial time. Therefore, we can conclude that
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

For
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, we can easily prove the claim using the definition
R(i,\mu,n)=R^{b}(L_{\mu}(n)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}i,\mu,n)
, the established results
\llceil{R^{b}|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
and
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, and the facts that deciding whether a string is a code of a number is possible in polynomial time and that
\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Finally, we leverage the above functions to show that
\llceil{G|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. The idea is to count the number of successor ordinals in the sequence of
n
-predecessors of the input ordinal as enumerated by
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
. For this purpose, define a function
F(x,u,v,w)
as follows: it returns
\bot
if it is not the case that

x=\left\lceil{y}\right\rceil
,
u=\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil
,
v=\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil
, and
w=\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil
for some
y,i,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
. Otherwise, set

F(\left\lceil{y}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)=\begin{cases}\left\lceil{y+1}\right\rceil&\text{if }\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)\text{ is a successor},\\
\left\lceil{y}\right\rceil&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, and since both the test for whether an ordinal is a successor and the computation of the numerical successor function are polynomial-time operations, it follows that
F\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
Next, define

g(u,v,w):=\begin{cases}\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil&\text{if }u=\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil,\\
F(g(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,v,w),u,v,w)&\text{if }u=\left\lceil{i+1}\right\rceil,\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is easy to see that
g(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)
counts the number of successor ordinals in the sequence
\{R(j,\mu,n)\}_{j=0}^{i}
. Therefore,

g(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{n}\right\rceil)\leq L_{\mu}(n),

for any
\mu\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the output of
g
is either
\bot
or a number bounded by
L_{\mu}(n)\leq M_{L}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|})
, we obtain

{|\!|}{{g(u,v,w)}}{|\!|}\leq 1+{|\!|}{{M_{L}({|\!|}{{v}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{w}}{|\!|})}}{|\!|}\leq 2+M_{L}({|\!|}{{v}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{w}}{|\!|}).

Since
M_{L}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we have
2+M_{L}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Therefore,
g
is defined by length-bounded primitive recursion and thus
g\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
by Theorem
2.8
.
Finally, observe that

\llceil{G|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(v,w)=g(\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(v,w),v,w).

Therefore, since
g
and
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil
are in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, we conclude that
\llceil{G|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
∎

9.2.2
The case
\Psi_{k}^{m}
, for
k,m\geq 1

In this subsubsection, we aim to show that
\llceil{R|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil_{E}
and
\llceil{L|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil_{E}

are computable in linear space and hence belong to
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
. As before, the main strategy is to establish upper bounds for
{|\!|}{{R(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}_{E}
and
L_{\mu}(n)
.
Recall from Definition
9.8

that
{|}{0}{|}=0
and
{|}{\alpha}{|}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}
, where
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}

is the normal form of the non-zero ordinal
\alpha\in\Psi_{\omega}
.
To obtain the desired upper bounds, we first express them in terms of
|\mu|
, and then, by applying Lemma
9.9
, we reformulate them in terms of
{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E}
.

Lemma 9.17
.

Let
k,m\in{\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}}
be natural numbers. Then:

(i)

There is a polynomial
P_{L}
with natural coefficients such that
