L_{\mu}(n)\leq P_{L}({|}{\mu}{|},n)
, for any
\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

There is a polynomial
P_{R}
with natural coefficients such that
{|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|}\allowbreak\leq P_{R}(i,{|}{\mu}{|},n)
,
for any
\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, define

P_{L}(x,n)=x\sum_{i=1}^{m+k}(n+2)^{i}
. We need to show that
L_{\mu}(n)\leq P_{L}({|}{\mu}{|},n)
. If
\mu=0
, as
L_{\mu}(n)=0
, there is nothing to prove. For non-zero
\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
, let
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\omega^{p_{i}}\cdot c_{i}
be its normal form. Note that for each
1\leq i\leq l
, by definition
c_{i}>0
and as
{|}{\mu}{|}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}
, we have
c_{i}\leq{|}{\mu}{|}
.
By Remark
6.20
,
l\leq m+k
. Therefore, using Lemma
4.5
and the fact that
\omega
and
c_{i}
’s are non-zero, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we reach:

L_{\mu}(n)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}(n+2)^{i}\leq{|}{\mu}{|}\sum_{i=1}^{l}(n+2)^{i}\leq{|}{\mu}{|}\sum_{i=1}^{m+k}(n+2)^{i}=P_{L}({|}{\mu}{|},n).

For
(ii)
, by Lemma
9.10
, we have
{|}{p(\alpha,n)}{|}\leq{|}{\alpha}{|}+n
, for any
\alpha\in\Psi^{m}_{k}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Define
P_{R}(i,x,n)=x+in
.
By induction on
i
, we prove
{|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|}\leq P_{R}(i,{|}{\mu}{|},n)
. If
i=0
, we have
R^{b}(0,\mu,n)=\mu
,
and the claim is clear. For the inductive case, assume the claim for
i
. Then, as
R^{b}(i+1,\mu,n)=p(R^{b}(i,\mu,n),n)
, we have

{|}{R^{b}(i+1,\mu,n)}{|}={|}{p(R^{b}(i,\mu,n),n)}{|}\leq{|}{\mu}{|}+in+n={|}{\mu}{|}+(i+1)n

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.18
.

For any polynomial
P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
with natural coefficients, there is a linear function
L
with natural coefficients such that for any
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\log(P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})+1)\leq L(\log(x_{1}+1),\ldots,\log(x_{l}+1))
.

Proof.

If
P(\bar{x})
is the zero polynomial, let
L=0
, and there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, let
P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}):=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{l}x_{j}^{e_{ij}}
, with
s\geq 1
, where each
e_{ij}\geq 0
. Consider the following inequalities. First, it is clear that
x^{e}+1\leq(x+2)^{e+1}
for any
x,e\in\mathbb{N}
. Second, since
a+b\leq ab
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 2}
, we obtain
\sum_{i=1}^{s}a_{i}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{s}a_{i}
for any
\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{s}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\geq 2}
. Third, as
(x+2)\leq 2(x+1)
, we have
\log(x+2)\leq\log(x+1)+1
. Using these inequalities, we obtain:

\log(P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})+1)\leq\log\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{l}(x_{j}^{e_{ij}}+1)\Big)\leq\log\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{l}(x_{j}+2)^{e_{ij}+1}\Big)

\leq\log\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{l}(x_{j}+2)^{e_{ij}+1}\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{l}{(e_{ij}+1)}\log(x_{j}+2)

\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{l}{(e_{ij}+1)}(\log(x_{j}+1)+1)\leq a\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{l}\log(x_{j}+1)+b

for sufficiently large constants
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Lemma 9.19
.

Let
k,m\in{\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}}
be natural numbers. Then, there are linear functions
M_{R}
and
M_{L}
with natural coefficients such that
{|\!|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|\!|}_{E}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{i}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{\mu}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}_{E})
and
{|\!|}{L_{\mu}(n)}{|\!|}_{E}\leq M_{L}({|\!|}{\mu}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}_{E})
,
for any
\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We only cover the case of
M_{R}
; the other is similar. By Lemma
9.17
, there is a polynomial
P_{R}
with natural coefficients such that

{|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|}\leq P_{R}(i,{|}{\mu}{|},n)
, for any

\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.
As
P_{R}
is a polynomial with natural coefficients, by Lemma
9.18
, there is a linear function
L
with natural coefficients such that:

\log({|}{R(i,\mu,n)}{|}+1)\leq\log(P_{R}(i,{|}{\mu}{|},n)+1)\leq L(\log(i+1),\log({|}{\mu}{|}+1),\log(n+1)).

By Lemma
9.9
, there are positive natural numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}
such that
\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)\leq{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}
and
{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}\leq a\cdot\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)+b
, for any
\alpha\in\Psi^{m}_{k}
. Define
M_{R}(x,y,z)=aL(x,y,z)+b
. Since
{|\!|}{x}{|\!|}_{E}-1\leq\log(x+1)\leq{|\!|}{x}{|\!|}_{E}
for any
x\in\mathbb{N}
, we reach
{|\!|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|\!|}_{E}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{\mu}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{n}{|\!|}_{E})
,
for any
\mu\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Theorem 9.20
.

Let
k,m\geq 1
be natural numbers. Then,
\llceil{R|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil_{E}
,
\llceil{L|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil_{E}
and
\llceil{G|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil_{E}

are all in
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
9.16
, replacing
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
with
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
, i.e., linear-space functions. The main points are as follows. First, by Lemma
9.7
, checking whether a string is a code of an ordinal in
\Psi^{m}_{k}
and, if so, whether it is zero, a successor, or a limit, can be done in linear space. Moreover, by the same lemma, the function
\llceil{p|_{\Psi^{m}_{k}}}\rrceil
is computable in linear space. Second, by Lemma
9.19
, there exist linear functions
M_{R}
and
M_{L}
with natural coefficients such that

{|\!|}{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}{|\!|}_{E}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}_{E})\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\mu}(n)\leq 2^{M_{L}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}_{E})}

for any
\mu\in\Psi^{m}_{k}
and any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
. These bounds are precisely what we need (see Theorem
2.8
) to apply the closure of
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}
under length-bounded recursion and bounded
\Sigma
-minimization.
∎

9.3
Bounding lemmas

In this subsection, we complete the third part of our strategy, as outlined in Section
7
, by establishing an upper bound on the length of the outputs of the functions in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{A}}
for
\mathsf{A}=\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
(with
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
) and
\mathsf{A}=\Psi_{k}^{m}
(with
k,m\geq 1
), using functions in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
and linear functions, respectively.

Lemma 9.21
.

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
. Then, for any
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}
, there is a monotone function
B_{f}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{i}a_{i},

for any
\bar{\mu}\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
and any
\bar{n},\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consequently,
there is a monotone function
E_{f}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

{|\!|}{{f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}}{|\!|}\leq E_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{n}}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{\bar{a}}{|\!|})
, for any
\bar{\mu}\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
and any
\bar{n},\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For the first part of the claim, we proceed by induction on the construction of
f
as an element of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}
.
For the base case, we define
B_{f}
as follows:

•

(constant) For the nullary zero function, take
B_{f}
as the constant
0
.

•

(projections) For any of the projections, take
B_{f}(\bar{x}):=\sum_{i}x_{i}
.

•

(successor)
Take
B_{f}
as the constant
1
.

•

(predecessor)
Take
B_{f}
as the constant
0
. It works
as
pred(;a)\leq a
.

•

(conditional)
Take
B_{f}
as the constant
0
.

For the safe composition, if
f
is constructed by:

f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})=h(\bar{\mu},\bar{s}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};);\bar{t}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})),

first, consider the functions
B_{h}
,
\bar{B}_{s}
, and
\bar{B}_{t}
obtained by using the induction hypothesis on
h
,
\bar{s}
, and
\bar{t}
, respectively. Then, define

B(\bar{x},\bar{y}):=B_{h}(\bar{x},\bar{B}_{s}(\bar{x},\bar{y}))
and set
B_{f}(\bar{x},\bar{y}):=B(\bar{x},\bar{y})+\sum_{i}B_{t_{i}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})
. Clearly
B_{f}
is monotone and in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Moreover, using the induction hypothesis and the monotonicity of
B_{h}
, we have:

\displaystyle h(\bar{\mu},\bar{s}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};);\bar{t}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a}))

\displaystyle\leq B_{h}\big({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{s}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};))+\max_{i}t_{i}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle\leq B_{h}\big({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{B}_{s}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})\big)+\max_{i}t_{i}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=B\big({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n}\big)+\max_{i}t_{i}(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle\leq B\big({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n}\big)+\max_{i}\big\{B_{t_{i}}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{j}a_{j}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq B_{f}\big({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n}\big)+\max_{j}a_{j}.

Therefore,
f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{j}a_{j}
.

For predicative ordinal recursion, assume that
f
is defined by

\displaystyle f(0,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=g(\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),

\displaystyle f(\mu+1,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{suc}}\!\big(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a}\big),

\displaystyle f(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{lim}}\!\big(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\mu_{q(\bar{n};)},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a}\big).

By the induction hypothesis, there exists
B_{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

q(\bar{n};)\leq B_{q}(\bar{n})
. By Lemma
9.11
, there are monotone
functions
M_{L},M_{R}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,n)}}{|\!|}\leq M_{R}({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|})\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\mu}(n)\leq M_{L}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{n}}{|\!|}),

for all
\mu\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
and
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
. Define

M_{L,q}(x,\bar{z}):=M_{L}(x,1+B_{q}(\bar{z})),\quad M_{R,q}(x,\bar{z}):=M_{R}(1+M_{L,q}(x,\bar{z}),x,1+B_{q}(\bar{z})).

By monotonicity of
M_{L}
and the fact that
{|\!|}{{b}}{|\!|}\leq 1+b
for all
b\in\mathbb{N}
,
we obtain

L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))\leq M_{L,q}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n}),\quad{|\!|}{{R^{b}(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))}}{|\!|}\leq M_{R,q}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n}).

Since this bound is independent of
i
, and since
R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
traverses the same sequence
of
q(\bar{n})
-predecessors of
\mu
, the same bound applies to
R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
.
Define

C(v,\bar{x},\bar{z}):=B_{h_{\mathsf{suc}}}(M_{R,q}(v,\bar{z}),\bar{x},\bar{z})+B_{h_{\mathsf{lim}}}(M_{R,q}(v,\bar{z}),\bar{x},\bar{z}),

and set

B_{f}(v,\bar{x},\bar{z}):=B_{g}(\bar{x},\bar{z})+M_{L,q}(v,\bar{z})\cdot C(v,\bar{x},\bar{z}).

It is immediate that
B_{f}
is monotone and
B_{f}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
.
For simplicity, write
\mu(i):=R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
. By the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle f(\mu(0),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=g(\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{g}({|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l},

\displaystyle f(\mu(i+1),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle\leq B_{H}({|\!|}{{\mu^{\prime}_{i}}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max\bigl\{f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\max_{l}a_{l}\bigr\},

where

(\mu^{\prime}_{i},H)=\begin{cases}(\mu(i),h_{\mathsf{suc}}),&\text{if $\mu(i+1)$ is a successor},\\
(\mu(i+1),h_{\mathsf{lim}}),&\text{if $\mu(i+1)$ is a limit}.\end{cases}

Since
\mu(j)=R(j,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
, we have

{|\!|}{{\mu(j)}}{|\!|}\leq M_{R,q}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n})
for all
j
,
and thus

B_{H}({|\!|}{{\mu^{\prime}_{i}}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})\leq C({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle f(\mu(0),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle\leq B_{g}({|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l},

\displaystyle f(\mu(i+1),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle\leq C({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max\{f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\max_{l}a_{l}\}.

By induction on
i
, it follows that

f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{g}({|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+i\,C({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l}.\qquad(*)

Recall that
L_{\alpha}(m)
is the least
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
R^{b}(j,\alpha,m)=0
.
Hence
R(L_{\alpha}(m),\alpha,m)=R^{b}(0,\alpha,m)=\alpha
which implies
R(L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n})),\mu,q(\bar{n}))=\mu
.
Since
\mu(L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n})))=R(L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n})),\mu,q(\bar{n}))
by definition, substituting
i=L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
into
(*)
and using

L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))\leq M_{L,q}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n})
, we conclude

f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{g}({|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+M_{L,q}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n})\,C({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l},

which implies

f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{f}({|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l}
.

If
f
is obtained by constant substitution, i.e.,

f(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i-1},\mu_{i+1},\ldots,\mu_{k},\bar{n};\bar{a})=g(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i-1},\alpha,\mu_{i+1},\ldots,\mu_{k},\bar{n};\bar{a}),

then, by the induction hypothesis, there exists monotone

B_{g}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}

such that

g(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq B_{g}({|\!|}{\bar{\mu}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{l}a_{l}.

It is then sufficient to define

B_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k},\bar{z}):=B_{g}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k},\bar{z}),

which is clearly monotone and belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

The case of structural rules follows similarly, namely by modifying in the obvious way
the function obtained via the induction hypothesis. This completes the proof of the first part.

For the second part, using the first part, the monotonicity of
B_{f}
, and the facts that
{|\!|}{{a}}{|\!|}\leq 1+a
and
m\leq 2^{{|\!|}{{m}}{|\!|}}
for every
a,m\in\mathbb{N}
, we reach

{|\!|}{{f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}}{|\!|}\leq{|\!|}{{B_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{i}a_{i}}}{|\!|}\leq 1+B_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},\bar{n})+\max_{i}a_{i}

\leq 1+B_{f}({|\!|}{{\bar{\mu}}}{|\!|},2^{{|\!|}{{\bar{n}}}{|\!|}})+\sum_{l}2^{{|\!|}{{a_{l}}}{|\!|}}.

It suffices now to observe that
E_{f}(\bar{x},\bar{y},\bar{z}):=1+B_{f}(\bar{x},\bar{2}^{\bar{y}})+\sum_{l}2^{z_{l}}
is monotone, belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{k}
as
k\geq 3
, and satisfies the required property.
∎

For
\mathsf{A}=\Psi_{k}^{m}
, where
k,m\geq 1
, we first bound the value of the function by a polynomial in terms of the numeral inputs and the norms of the ordinal inputs, rather than the lengths of their encodings. We then apply Lemma
9.9
and Lemma
9.18
to connect
{|}{\mu}{|}
with
{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E}
via a logarithmic relationship, which in turn yields a linear bound on the length of the functions.

Lemma 9.22
.

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}
. For any

f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}
,
there is
a polynomial
P_{f}
with natural coefficients such that

f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq P_{f}({|}{\bar{\mu}}{|},\bar{n})+\max_{i}{a_{i}}
,
for any
\bar{\mu}\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
\bar{n},\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The argument is analogous to that of Lemma
9.21
.
The only difference is that, in this case, we use Lemma
9.17
to polynomially bound

{|}{R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))}{|}
and
L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
in terms of
{|}{\mu}{|}
and
\bar{n}
.
∎

Corollary 9.23
.

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. Then, for every

f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}
, there exists a linear function

L_{f}
with natural coefficients such that

{|\!|}{f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}{|\!|}_{E}\;\leq\;L_{f}\bigl({|\!|}{\bar{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E},\,{|\!|}{\bar{n}}{|\!|}_{E},\,{|\!|}{\bar{a}}{|\!|}_{E}\bigr),

for all
\bar{\mu}\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and all

\bar{n},\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.22
, there is a polynomial function

P_{f}
with natural coefficients such that

f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\leq P_{f}({|}{\bar{\mu}}{|},\bar{n},\bar{a})
,
for any
\bar{\mu}\in\Psi_{k}^{m}
and any
\bar{n},\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, by Lemma
9.18
,
there is a linear map
L
with natural coefficients
such that

\log\bigl(1+f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\bigr)\leq\log\bigl(1+P_{f}({|}{\bar{\mu}}{|},\bar{n},\bar{a})\bigr)\leq L(\log({|}{\bar{\mu}}{|}+1),\log(\bar{n}+1),\log(\bar{a}+1)).

By Lemma
9.9
,

\log({|}{\alpha}{|}+1)\leq{|\!|}{\alpha}{|\!|}_{E}
, for any
\alpha\in\Psi^{m}_{k}
. Moreover, note that
{|\!|}{x}{|\!|}_{E}-1\leq\log(x+1)\leq{|\!|}{x}{|\!|}_{E}
for any
x\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
L_{f}(x,y,z):=L(x,y,z)+1
. As
L_{f}
has natural coefficients, both
L
and, consequently,
L_{f}
are monotone. Therefore:

{|\!|}{f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}{|\!|}_{E}\leq\log\bigl(1+f(\bar{\mu},\bar{n};\bar{a})\bigr)+1\leq L_{f}({|\!|}{\bar{\mu}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{\bar{n}}{|\!|}_{E},{|\!|}{\bar{a}}{|\!|}_{E}),

which completes the proof.
∎

9.4
Proof of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}

In this subsection, we employ all the ingredients developed so far to show that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}
, for
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
, and
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
, for
k,m\geq 1
, and then conclude that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
, for any bounded
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
.

Lemma 9.24
.

(i)

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 3
and
m\geq 1
. Then,

\llceil{f}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

(ii)

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}^{\geq 1}
. Then,

\llceil{f}\rrceil_{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\circ}

for any

f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}
. Therefore,

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, we proceed by induction on the construction of
f
as an element of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}
. The claim for the basic functions is immediate.
For the inductive step, if
f
is constructed by safe composition, the claim follows directly from the induction hypothesis and the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
is closed under composition.
If
f
is defined by the following predicative ordinal recursion:

\displaystyle f(0,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=g(\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle f(\mu+1,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{suc}}(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\eta,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a})

\displaystyle f(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathsf{lim}}(\langle\mu_{i}\rangle_{i},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};f(\mu_{q(\bar{n};)},\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),\bar{a}),

by the induction hypothesis, the functions
\llceil{q}\rrceil
,
\llceil{g}\rrceil
,
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{suc}}}\rrceil
, and
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{lim}}}\rrceil
are all in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.
To prove that
\llceil{f}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, we begin with an informal explanation of our strategy, ignoring the encoding of ordinals and numbers. For simplicity, denote
R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))
by
\mu(i)
. Observe that the recursion in the definition of
f(\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
actually operates on the sequence
\{\mu(i)\}_{i=0}^{L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))}
of
q(\bar{n})
-predecessors of
\mu
. Hence, it suffices to construct
f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
as a function of
i
and then substitute
i=L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
. We already know that the function
\llceil{L|_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}}\rrceil
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. Define

F(i,\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n},\bar{a})
as
f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a}),

and notice that
F
is constructible by a primitive recursion on
i
, using
g
,
h_{\mathsf{suc}}
, and
h_{\mathsf{lim}}
. Finally, by Lemma
9.21
, the length of
F(i,\mu,\bar{\nu},\bar{n},\bar{a})
is bounded by a function in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
in terms of
{|\!|}{{\mu(i)}}{|\!|}
,
{|\!|}{{\bar{\nu}}}{|\!|}
,
{|\!|}{{\bar{n}}}{|\!|}
, and
{|\!|}{{\bar{a}}}{|\!|}
. Since
{|\!|}{{\mu(i)}}{|\!|}
can be bounded in terms of
{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|}
,
{|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|}
, and
{|\!|}{{\bar{n}}}{|\!|}
by a function in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we may conclude, by applying the closure of
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
under length-bounded primitive recursion, that
F\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, which completes the proof.

In the following, we implement this strategy in detail, bringing in the encoding of ordinals and numbers. First, recall from Lemma
9.4
that deciding whether a string in
\Sigma^{*}
encodes an ordinal in
\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
, and if so, whether it is zero, a successor, or a limit, can be done in polynomial time. Moreover, the same lemma ensures that

\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, as
k\geq 3
.
Now, define a function
I(b)
on
\Sigma^{*}
which outputs
b
if
b=\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil
for some
\alpha\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
, and
\bot
otherwise. Next, anticipating that
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{suc}}}\rrceil
at
\left\lceil{\alpha+1}\right\rceil
uses
\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil
, and
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{lim}}}\rrceil
at
\left\lceil{\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle}\right\rceil
uses
\left\lceil{\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle}\right\rceil
itself,
define
S(b)
on
\Sigma^{*}
by

S(b)=\begin{cases}\llceil{p|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(b,\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil),&\text{if }b=\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil\text{ for some successor ordinal }\alpha\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3},\\[4.0pt]
I(b),&\text{if }b=\left\lceil{\alpha}\right\rceil\text{ for some limit ordinal }\alpha\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3},\\[4.0pt]
\bot,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

From the above discussion, it follows that both
I
and
S
belong to
\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Now we define a function responsible for the recursive calls that determine
f(\mu(i+1),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
in terms of
f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
, for any
i<L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
. The task is simply to decide whether
\mu(i+1)
is a successor or a limit, and then apply either
h_{\mathsf{suc}}
or
h_{\mathsf{lim}}
.

To implement this idea, in the presence of the codings, first define the relation
B(y,u,\bar{w})
on
\Sigma^{*}
stating the existence of
i,\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in\Phi_{k-3}^{m}
such that
y=\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil
,
u=\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil
,
\bar{w}=\left\lceil{\bar{n}}\right\rceil
and
i<L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
. By Lemma
9.4
, Theorem
9.16
, and the induction hypothesis, the relation
B
is computable in space
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Now, define

J(y,u,\bar{v},\bar{w},x,\bar{z}):=\begin{cases}H\bigl(S(\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(y,u,\llceil{q}\rrceil(\bar{w}))),\bar{v},\bar{w},x,\bar{z}\bigr),&B(y,u,\bar{w}),\\[4.0pt]
\bot,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

where
H
is
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{suc}}}\rrceil
if

\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(y,u,\llceil{q}\rrceil(\bar{w}))

is a code for a successor ordinal, and
H
is
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{lim}}}\rrceil
otherwise.
This function
J
is clearly in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
by the induction hypothesis,
Lemma
9.4
, and Theorem
9.16
.

Intuitively,
y
holds the code of the index
i
,
x
holds the code of the value of the recursive call to
\llceil{f}\rrceil
at each step, and
u,\bar{v},\bar{w},
and
\bar{z}
hold the codes of the inputs of
\llceil{f}\rrceil
, namely
\lceil\mu\rceil
,
\lceil\bar{\nu}\rceil
,
\lceil\bar{n}\rceil
, and
\lceil\bar{a}\rceil
, respectively. Thus, for
i<L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
, the function
J
is responsible for invoking either
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{suc}}}\rrceil
or
\llceil{h_{\mathsf{lim}}}\rrceil
, depending on whether
\mu(i+1)
is a successor or a limit.

Now we can define
f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})
using primitive recursion on
i
. Formally, define

F(y,u,\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z}):=\begin{cases}\llceil{g}\rrceil(\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z}),&\text{if }y=\left\lceil{0}\right\rceil,\\[6.0pt]
J(y,u,\bar{v},\bar{w},F(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,u,\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z}),\bar{z}),&\text{if }y=\left\lceil{i+1}\right\rceil,\\[6.0pt]
\bot,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is not hard to see that

F(\left\lceil{i}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\mu}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\bar{\nu}}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\bar{n}}\right\rceil,\left\lceil{\bar{a}}\right\rceil)=\left\lceil{f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}\right\rceil,\qquad(*)

for any
i\leq L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
.

To show that
F\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
, it suffices to find an upper bound for the length of
F
. For this purpose, by Theorem
9.16
, we have
\llceil{R|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. Therefore, it is computable in time
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, which means that there exists a monotone function
N\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that

{|\!|}{{R(i,\alpha,m)}}{|\!|}\leq N({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\alpha}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{m}}{|\!|})

for any
\alpha\in\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
and any
m,i\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, by Lemma
9.21
for
q
and the monotonicity of
N
, we reach

{|\!|}{{R(i,\mu,q(\bar{n}))}}{|\!|}\leq N({|\!|}{{i}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{{\mu}}{|\!|},E_{q}({|\!|}{{\bar{n}}}{|\!|})).

Now, since the value of
F
is either
\bot
or
\left\lceil{f(\mu(i),\bar{\nu},\bar{n};\bar{a})}\right\rceil
, for some
i\leq L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
, by Lemma
9.21
we have the following bound:

{|\!|}{{F(y,u,\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z})}}{|\!|}\leq 1+E_{f}\bigl(N({|\!|}{{y}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{u}{|\!|},E_{q}({|\!|}{\bar{w}}{|\!|})),{|\!|}{\bar{v}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{\bar{w}}{|\!|},{|\!|}{\bar{z}}{|\!|}\bigr).

Since
E_{f},E_{q},N\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
is closed under length-bounded primitive recursion by Theorem
2.8
, we conclude that
F\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Finally, substituting
i=L_{\mu}(q(\bar{n}))
in
(*)
, and noting that
F
returns
\bot
for any inputs that are not codes of ordinals in
\Phi^{m}_{k-3}
or numbers, we obtain

\llceil{f}\rrceil(u,\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z})=F(\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil(u,\llceil{q}\rrceil(\bar{w})),u,\bar{v},\bar{w},\bar{z}).

By Theorem
9.16
,
\llceil{L|_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. Therefore, we conclude that
\llceil{f}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Finally, since the cases where
f
is obtained by constant substitution or structural rules are straightforward, we can conclude that for any
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}
, we have
\llceil{f}\rrceil\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. This completes the first part of
(i)
. For the second part, since functions in
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi^{m}_{k-3}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{k-3}^{m}}
have no ordinal inputs, they are numeral functions whose lifts are in
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\circ}
. Therefore, by Remark
2.7
, they must be in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

The proof of part
(ii)
is similar to that of
(i)
. It suffices to use linear-space algorithms and the linear bounds from Lemma
9.7
, Theorem
9.20
, and Corollary
9.23
.
∎

Theorem 9.25
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
be a bounded downset of ordinals. Then
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
.

Proof.

First, we prove that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k+2}
for any
k\geq 1
and that
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{\omega}}\allowbreak\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
. For the first claim, note that, by Lemma
9.24
, for any
m\geq 1
, we have
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi^{m}_{k-1}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k+2}
.
Next, by Lemma
6.9
, we have
\Phi_{k}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\Phi^{m}_{k-1}
and
\Phi_{k-1}^{m}\subseteq\Phi^{m+1}_{k-1}
for all
m\geq 1
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.13
, it follows that

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k}}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi^{m}_{k-1}}
.
Consequently,
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{k+2}
.
For the second claim, for any

k\geq 1
and
m\geq 1
, we have

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
, by Lemma
9.24
.
Now, by Lemma
6.12
,

\Psi_{k}^{m}\subseteq\Psi_{k}^{m+1}
, for any
k,m\geq 1
. Moreover, for any
k\geq 1
, we have
\Psi_{k}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\Psi_{k}^{m}
.
Thus, by Lemma
5.13
, we reach
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}^{m}}
which implies
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
Similarly,
we have that

\Psi_{k}\subseteq\Psi_{k+1}

for any
k\geq 1

and
\Psi_{\omega}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Psi_{k}
, by Lemma
6.12
. Thus,

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{\omega}}=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{k}}
. Therefore,

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Psi_{\omega}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

Now, if
l(\mathsf{A})\geq 1
, then by definition we have
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})}
. Hence, by Lemma
5.12
, it follows that

\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{l(\mathsf{A})}}
.
Therefore, setting
k=l(\mathsf{A})
in the above claim yields

\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2}
.
If
l(\mathsf{A})=0
, by definition
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Psi_{\omega}
. By the same line of argument, we reach
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
∎

10
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we generalized Bellantoni-Cook’s predicative recursive functions by extending their predicative recursion on natural numbers to predicative ordinal recursion on any ordinal in a given downset
\mathsf{A}
of constructive ordinals. We denote this class of functions by
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
. We then introduced the downset
\Phi_{\omega}
(resp.
\Phi_{k}
) of constructive ordinals as the constructive counterpart of the set-theoretic ordinals below
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{0})
(resp.
\bm{\phi}_{k}(\bm{0})
), where
\bm{\phi}_{k}
is the
k
th Veblen function. Finally, we established a complete classification of
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
for any downset
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Phi_{\omega}
that contains at least one infinite constructive ordinal. More precisely, we showed that for any such downset, if
\mathsf{A}
is not contained in any
\Phi_{k}
, then
\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}
coincides with the class of all primitive recursive functions; otherwise,

\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{A}}\;=\;\mathcal{E}_{l(\mathsf{A})+2},

where
l(\mathsf{A})
is a natural number measuring the level of
\mathsf{A}
in
\Phi_{\omega}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{k}
denotes the
k
th level of the Grzegorczyk hierarchy. In particular, we obtained a machine-independent and structural characterization of
\mathcal{E}_{k}
as

\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{k-2}}
for any
k\geq 3
. For
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we generalized Bellantoni-Cook’s characterization to

\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{\omega}}}=\mathrm{PredR}_{\mathsf{D}_{\omega^{m}}}
for any
m\geq 2
, where
\mathsf{D}_{\alpha}
is the set of all constructive ordinals strictly below
\alpha
. This shows that even if we extend predicative ordinal recursion from
\omega
to ordinals below
\omega^{m}
for
m\geq 2
, or to any constructive ordinal below
\omega^{\omega}
, the resulting functions still belong to
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

For future work, we outline three directions. First, we aim to extend the present classification beyond
\Phi_{\omega}
to encompass broader classes of constructive ordinals.
Alternatively,
this may be viewed as extending
the characterization of the
\mathcal{E}_{k}
’s to
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
for infinite
\alpha
. Second, by using a suitable form of predicative recursion on arbitrary well-founded partial orders
[
27
]
, we conjecture that the predicatively recursive functions on a well-founded partial order correspond to the predicatively recursive functions on its associated ordinal (i.e., its height). Proving this conjecture
would generalize the main classification of this paper to all well-founded structures whose height is below
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{0})
. Third, we plan to develop the arithmetical theories corresponding to these predicative ordinal recursive functions. These absolutely predicative theories of arithmetic
would be
defined in terms of different comprehension schemes. A similar approach, using separation in the types of involved terms, has already been proposed and studied
[
37
]
. After developing such theories, we intend to apply the machinery of ordinal analysis to them, with the aim of characterizing their provably total recursive functions in terms of predicative ordinal recursive functions.
For instance, a predicative arithmetical theory with
\Delta^{0}_{\infty}
-comprehension can be regarded as the absolutely predicative version of
\mathsf{PA}
. Therefore, we expect its proof-theoretic ordinal to be
\bm{\epsilon_{0}}
and, since
\Phi_{1}
is the set of constructive representations of ordinals below
\bm{\epsilon_{0}}
, the provably recursive functions of the theory must correspond to
\mathrm{PredR}_{\Phi_{1}}=\mathcal{E}_{3}
, i.e., the Kalmár elementary functions. This is consistent with similar results obtained for other proposals of absolutely predicative versions of Peano arithmetic; see
[
37
]
.
